
=== PDF PAGE 1 ===
AI Benchmark Half-Life in Recursive Corpora:
A Theory of Validity Decay under Semantic Leakage and Regeneration
K. Takahashi
ORCID: 0009-0004-4273-3365
March 11, 2026
Abstract
Modern AI benchmarks no longer live outside the training and evaluation ecosystem. They
live inside recursive corpora, where benchmark items, paraphrases, explanations, model outputs,
benchmark-aware curation rules, and judge conventions re-enter the public knowledge stream and
can later affect both models and evaluators. Existing literatures on recursive training, contamination
detection, dynamic benchmark construction, construct validity, benchmark saturation, and human–AI
co-produced knowledge each capture part of this problem, but they do not yet provide a unified
lifetime theory for benchmarks as measurement instruments. This paper develops an origin-free
theory of benchmark half-life: the first time a benchmark lineage loses half of its initial validity
under a declared operational evaluation specification. We reject nominal data origin (“human” versus
“synthetic”) as a primitive because origin labels are often latent, unstable under mixed production, and
neither necessary nor sufficient for benchmark quality. Instead, we model benchmark decay through
observable or estimable state variables: semantic leakage, verified novelty inflow, lineage observability,
verification fidelity, support diversity, evaluator coupling, and construct drift. Validity is defined relative
to an explicit operational class and decomposed into discriminative power and a construct-validity
component that combines resolved-pair mass with thresholded order fidelity. We separate weak passive￾envelope assumptions, which suffice for intervalwise retirement bounds and cross-ecology ordering,
from stronger state-reduced laws, which suffice for statewise comparison, proxy-based identification,
factor-targeted experimentation, and control once declared regime-time jumps are recorded explicitly.
The framework is jump aware: passive hazard statements apply only between declared benchmark
interventions, while validity envelopes and identification formulas carry separate multiplicative jump
factors. This keeps first-hitting-time objects, regeneration, and safe control logically compatible. The
paper then develops anchor-based construct-validity identification under scalar, directional, abstention,
and nondifferential class-conditional error models; portfolio design and monitoring under common-mode
decay and uncertain coverage; and safe sequential monitoring and control under partial observability,
transition-kernel ambiguity, frontier-pruned portfolio actions, and a joint estimation-to-control certificate.
The practical implication is that benchmark reports should carry operational scope, identification
status, validity intervals, dependence budgets, exclusion logic, jump logs, coverage uncertainty budgets,
coverage-frontier certificates, ambiguity sets for control, estimation-to-control validity budgets, and
life-cycle status, not raw scores alone.

1 Introduction
Benchmarks are now treated as public instruments of scientific and engineering progress in AI. They anchor
model comparisons, guide model release narratives, and increasingly influence procurement, deployment,
and policy decisions. Yet the environment in which these instruments operate has changed. A benchmark
9

=== PDF PAGE 10 ===
no longer sits cleanly outside the corpus ecology. Instead, benchmark items, paraphrases, solution traces,
model-generated explanations, benchmark-aware filtering heuristics, and community discussions circulate
through the same public channels that later model builders, evaluators, and downstream users consume.
The benchmark has become one component of a recursive corpus.
Several adjacent literatures already illuminate parts of this landscape. Recursive training can induce
model collapse when generated data recursively replace real data, especially in distribution tails [1, 2]. But
collapse is not inevitable: when synthetic data accumulate alongside real data, degradation can be reduced
or avoided [3]. Contamination studies have shown that benchmark overlap inflates apparent performance
and that detection is harder than exact-match filtering suggests [4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9]. Dynamic benchmarks
such as LiveBench, AntiLeak-Bench, and LiveXiv respond by refreshing questions, relying on objective
answers, and injecting temporally new content [10, 11, 12, 13]. Measurement-oriented work argues that
even uncontaminated benchmarks may fail to measure the intended construct [14, 15, 16, 17]. More recent
studies show both soft contamination by semantic duplicates and variant contamination beyond exact
overlap [18, 19] and benchmark saturation as models converge toward empirical ceilings [20]. Human–AI
collective-knowledge models add a further complication: the open web is increasingly co-produced by
humans and LLMs, blurring any stable boundary between human-origin and AI-origin text [21].
These lines of work are individually useful but jointly incomplete. Recursive-training papers study
training-distribution degradation, not the lifespan of benchmarks as instruments. Contamination papers
study overlap and detection, not the joint evolution of discriminative power and construct validity.
Dynamic benchmark papers give design recipes, not a general decay law. Construct-validity papers explain
why score-based claims can fail, but not when benchmarks should be retired or regenerated. Saturation
studies quantify plateauing, but do not integrate semantic leakage, evaluator coupling, construct drift,
and regeneration into a single dynamical quantity.
This paper proposes that quantity. We define benchmark validity as the product of discriminative
power and a construct-validity component. The first measures whether the benchmark still separates
relevant systems above the declared score-noise floor. The second combines two logically distinct issues:
whether the operational class still contains enough target-resolved pairs to matter, and whether the
benchmark preserves the target ordering on those resolved pairs once score-resolution limits are respected.
The benchmark half-life is then the first time this validity falls below half of its initial value. The
resulting theory is deliberately origin-free. We do not use “human” and “synthetic” as primitive ontological
categories because they are increasingly latent, unstable, and poorly aligned with what actually harms or
preserves benchmark quality. A high-novelty synthetic stream can be instrumentally useful; a low-novelty
human-origin stream can still erode a benchmark through exposure and derivative leakage. The relevant
primitives are semantic leakage, verified novelty inflow, verification fidelity, support diversity, evaluator
coupling, construct drift, and provenance observability.
The contributions are organized into four blocks. First, the paper gives an origin-free mathematical
setup for recursive benchmark ecology, clarifies operational-scope dependence, and separates passive decay
from explicit benchmark revisions or regeneration jumps. Second, it develops decay and identification
theory: envelope and statewise comparison results, finite-lifetime and survival guarantees, non-identification
theorems for score traces and aggregate validity, calibrated-support identification results, off-support
extrapolation results under weak shape restrictions, and anchor-based partial-identification results for
the construct-validity component under resolved-set mismatch, directional mismatch sharpening, anchor
dependence, and qualification-abstention. Third, it extends the analysis from single benchmarks to
10

=== PDF PAGE 11 ===
benchmark portfolios with common-mode hazards, uncertain coverage matrices, and learned uncertainty
sets under dependent or nonstationary audits. Fourth, it connects lifetime estimation to governance by
deriving anytime-valid monitoring rules, safe retirement and regeneration control, robust ambiguity-set
control, robust belief-state control under partial observability, an explicit estimation-to-control interface,
and approximate safe-control regret bounds.
The theory is intentionally modest. It does not claim that all benchmark decay is captured by a single
scalar, nor that origin metadata are useless. Rather, it argues that benchmark life-cycle governance should
be based on state variables and uncertainty bounds, not on raw scores or nominal source labels alone.
Reader roadmap. The paper is organized so that readers can stop at the weakest layer that answers
their use case. Sections 3 and 5 introduce the operational objects and give intervalwise lifetime results that
need only passive-envelope control. Section 6 adds stronger assumptions only where point identification,
factor attribution, or anchor sharpening require them. Section 7 lifts the analysis from single benchmarks
to benchmark families with uncertain coverage. Section 8 then adds sequential monitoring and safe
control, including robust Bellman recursions for both information-state and belief-state ambiguity,
conservative outer-hull reductions for nonrectangular ambiguity, and coverage-frontier action pruning.
Readers interested mainly in implementation can jump from the setup directly to Sections 9 and 11.
2 Related Work and Positioning
2.1 Recursive training and model collapse
Shumailov et al. showed that recursively training on generated data can distort distributions and erase
tails, producing what they called the curse of recursion [1, 2]. Gerstgrasser et al. then showed that collapse
is not inevitable if real data are continually accumulated alongside synthetic data [3]. These papers are
crucial but they address the health of training distributions, not the lifetime of benchmarks as evaluative
instruments. Our object is benchmark validity, not corpus fidelity alone.
2.2 Benchmark contamination and detection
A growing literature surveys contamination definitions, detectors, and remediation strategies [4, 5, 6, 7].
Samuel et al. emphasize inconsistencies and oracle challenges in modern contamination detection [8].
Singh et al. propose ConTAM and argue that contamination metrics should be judged by whether the
flagged examples actually benefit models downstream [9]. Liang et al. introduce DVD, a detector aimed
specifically at variant contamination, where semantically equivalent items evade simpler matching rules
[19]. This literature is highly relevant to our estimation protocol, but its center of gravity is detection
quality rather than benchmark lifetime dynamics.
2.3 Dynamic benchmarks and regeneration
LiveBench, AntiLeak-Bench, and LiveXiv exemplify the move from static to dynamic evaluation [10, 11,
12]. Chen et al. survey this transition and make clear that the field is moving toward time-sensitive,
contamination-aware benchmark maintenance rather than purely static test sets [13]. These works motivate
our regeneration variable, but they do not yield a general state-variable theory of how much regeneration
is enough to control benchmark half-life.
11

=== PDF PAGE 12 ===
2.4 Adaptive reuse, dynamic creation, and psychometric benchmarking
Adaptive data analysis showed earlier that repeatedly reusing an evaluation set can itself destroy inferential
validity, even without any ontological change in the data source [27, 28]. This is conceptually adjacent
to benchmark half-life: repeated exposure and adaptation can make an apparently fixed instrument
progressively less trustworthy. Dynabench operationalizes dynamic, human-and-model-in-the-loop
benchmark creation, where annotators actively search for examples that current models fail on [29].
More recent psychometric and adaptive approaches such as Fluid Benchmarking use item-response
structure and dynamic item selection to improve validity and reduce saturation [30]. These works strongly
motivate our emphasis on reuse pressure, regeneration, and operational-class-relative validity, but they do
not by themselves provide a general hazard-based lifetime theory.
2.5 Partial identification and interval inference
In settings with interval-valued or incompletely observed variables, econometrics treats many targets
as partially identified rather than point identified [31, 32, 37]. That stance is directly relevant here.
Recursive-corpus contamination, provenance, and benchmark-coupling states are often observed only
through noisy detectors, incomplete logs, or heuristic proxies. Our interval results therefore adopt a
deliberately conservative logic: absent an injective and calibrated observation model, benchmark validity
and half-life should be reported as identification regions rather than faux-precision point estimates.
For projected-support extrapolation, the relevant comparison class is not unrestricted smooth curve
fitting but shape-restricted extension—for example Lipschitz or monotone continuation from calibrated
support, in the spirit of classical extension results such as McShane’s theorem [41]. Design-based exclusion
and instrumental-variables logic are also relevant once one moves from passive monitoring to factor
attribution: with the right exclusion restrictions, aggregate responses can identify otherwise hidden
structural components [42]. When those exclusions are only approximate, Conley–Hansen–Rossi style
plausibly exogenous logic becomes the right comparison class: one should report sensitivity regions indexed
by declared violation budgets rather than pretend the instrument is pristine [47].
2.6 Latent-panel and annotator-error models
Anchor panels are measurement devices with their own error structure. Classical latent-annotator models
such as Dawid–Skene estimate observer error rates from repeated noisy labels without direct access
to ground truth [38]. That tradition motivates our insistence that anchor quality and dependence be
modeled explicitly rather than smuggled in as an unexamined source of “expert” truth. Our latent-factor
panel result is deliberately weaker than a full Dawid–Skene model: it does not try to identify every
anchor-specific confusion matrix, only to upper-bound construct-validity error under common latent
difficulty or blind-spot structure.
2.7 Portfolio design and robust allocation
Once benchmark half-life is extended from a single instrument to a benchmark family, allocation questions
become unavoidable. Classical portfolio theory studies allocation under known or estimated return￾risk trade-offs [39], while robust optimization studies allocation under uncertainty sets and worst-case
guarantees [40, 45]. Tail-risk functionals such as conditional value-at-risk provide another lens for allocation
when one cares about rare but severe failures rather than only mean performance [46]. Data-driven
12

=== PDF PAGE 13 ===
robust optimization adds a further lesson that matters directly here: uncertainty sets can be learned from
audit data and can carry finite-sample transfer guarantees when the true object lands inside the learned
set with known confidence [48]. Our benchmark-portfolio design results are much simpler than those
literatures in objective and constraints, but they inherit the same conceptual lesson: diversification is
only rational once one acknowledges uncertainty, common-mode exposure, uncertain coverage accounting,
learned uncertainty sets, or explicit breadth constraints. Under a single known deterministic hazard
profile, the optimal horizon-survival design collapses to the best single benchmark.
2.8 Sequential monitoring and anytime-valid inference
Online benchmark governance is a sequential decision problem because scores, audits, and contamination
evidence arrive over time while stakeholders keep peeking. Confidence-sequence and always-valid inference
frameworks therefore provide the right comparison class for retirement and continuation rules under
optional stopping: one wants intervals or envelopes that remain valid no matter when monitoring stops
[49, 50]. Our sequential monitoring extension does not derive new confidence sequences from first principles;
it shows how any declared anytime-valid validity envelope can be converted into safe retirement and
continuation rules for benchmark governance and then into a constrained dynamic control problem for
retirement versus regeneration.
2.9 Dependent auditing, mixing, and martingale concentration
Once audit rows are clustered by time, evaluator, family, or provenance chain, independence becomes
a decorative fiction. Strong-mixing surveys such as Bradley’s show both the usefulness and the limits
of classical dependence regimes [51]. On the martingale side, Freedman-type inequalities and later time￾uniform concentration tools show how predictable variation can replace independence as the relevant
control variable for sequential inference [52, 49]. Our dependence-aware learned-set results borrow this
viewpoint: the relevant question is not whether audits are independent in a metaphysical sense, but
whether one has a valid sequential uncertainty construction under the actual dependence structure.
2.10 Nonstationary audits and regime switching
Dependence is only part of the story. Audit processes can also be nonstationary because benchmark
taxonomies drift, evaluator pools change, or benchmark families move between different operating regimes.
Classical regime-switching time-series models show how piecewise or latent changes in data-generating
structure can be modeled without collapsing everything into one stationary law [53]. Our nonstationary
audit extension adopts the same sober lesson: if audit regimes change, the uncertainty set should track
the current or posterior-weighted regime rather than pretending that one fixed coverage matrix governs
all times.
2.11 POMDP control and exploration–exploitation
The control side is naturally close to partially observed Markov decision processes, because the true
benchmark-ecology state is rarely observed directly. Standard POMDP results show that optimal control
can be written on the belief state rather than the latent state itself [58]. Robust MDP work shows that
stagewise rectangular ambiguity over transition kernels can be propagated through a max–min Bellman
13

=== PDF PAGE 14 ===
recursion rather than hidden behind a single plug-in estimate [54, 55]. Robust and distributionally
robust POMDP work extends the same lesson to partially observed systems, where ambiguity can sit
on the joint transition–observation law and still admit belief-state dynamic programming under suitable
structure [56, 57]. Once learning and action are coupled, one also enters the exploration–exploitation
territory studied in reinforcement learning, where confidence-based optimism can deliver finite regret
guarantees under explicit uncertainty accounting [59]. Our extension borrows these lessons but keeps the
benchmark-governance twist: the action set is safety-trimmed by anytime-valid validity bounds before any
exploration is allowed, the ambiguity set is an explicit reported object, and the estimation layer is tied to
control through a joint fail-closed certificate rather than a silent handoff.
2.12 Approximate dynamic programming and finite-horizon control
Once retirement and regeneration are treated as a sequential decision problem, exact dynamic programming
quickly becomes expensive on realistic information states. Approximate dynamic programming and neuro￾dynamic programming provide the natural comparison class [60]. Finite-sample and performance analyses
for approximate value iteration and fitted value iteration show how value-function error propagates into
policy loss [61]. Our safe-control extension is simpler than those general frameworks because the action
space is explicitly safety-trimmed by the anytime-valid envelope, but the same moral applies: approximate
control should come with explicit residual or regret certificates rather than hand-wavy optimization
theater.
2.13 Construct validity, psychometrics, and model metrology
The construct-validity component used here sits inside a much older measurement tradition. Cronbach
and Meehl framed construct validity as the disciplined relation between test behavior, theory, and the
inferential claims a measurement instrument is meant to support [33]. Messick later unified validity under
an evidential and consequential interpretation, emphasizing that score meaning cannot be detached from
intended use [34]. Measurement-oriented critiques in AI now make a parallel point. Bean et al. show that
many benchmark claims rest on weak construct-validity foundations [14]. Saxon et al. call for model
metrology rather than scoreboard-centric benchmarking [15]. Salaudeen et al. provide a validity-centered
framework linking evidence to evaluative claims [16]. Vendrow et al. show that even benchmark labels
and adjudications can be systematically wrong enough to distort reliability claims, motivating more
carefully curated “platinum” benchmarks [23]. Welty et al. earlier argued that AI evaluation should
import metrological thinking about instrument resolution and uncertainty [17]. Our paper is squarely in
this tradition, but adds explicit decay dynamics and a half-life object.
2.14 Item response theory and dynamic discrimination
Item-response theory studies how test items discriminate among latent ability levels and how item quality
changes with item difficulty and population mix [35]. That perspective is conceptually adjacent to our
discriminative-power term: both ask whether an instrument still separates the operational class it is meant
to resolve. The difference is that we study the temporal decay of an entire benchmark lineage under
recursive exposure, regeneration, and construct drift rather than the static calibration of individual items.
Still, IRT provides an important antecedent for thinking about saturation, operational-class dependence,
and the distinction between item difficulty and instrument validity.
14

=== PDF PAGE 15 ===
2.15 Social choice and rank aggregation
Benchmark orderings are also related to social-choice and rank-aggregation problems. Arrow’s classical
work shows that there is no frictionless way to aggregate heterogeneous pairwise information into a
universally satisfactory ordering rule [36]. That is not the same problem studied here, but it clarifies why
construct-validity auditing cannot rely on one benchmark, one anchor, or one judge as if they revealed
a uniquely privileged ranking. Our anchor-panel and mismatch results should therefore be read partly
as metrological analogues of rank-aggregation robustness: the question is not only who wins under one
ordering device, but how stable the inferred order is under noisy and partially overlapping comparison
systems.
2.16 Design-based causal inference and matched intervention logic
The intervention-design part of the paper is also aligned with design-based causal inference. Matched
designs, exclusion restrictions, and local instrumental-variables logic are standard ways to recover causal
structure when direct observation is insufficient [42, 43, 44]. Our factor-targeted intervention results adapt
that design logic to benchmark ecology: matched contrasts, exclusion budgets, and structural spillover
classes are not rhetorical decoration but the objects that determine what can and cannot be identified
from aggregate validity traces.
2.17 Benchmark-quality self-assessment and adversarial benchmarking
Some recent work evaluates benchmarks themselves rather than only the models scored on them. Qian et
al. propose Benchmark2
, which measures ranking consistency, discriminability, and capability-alignment
deviation across benchmark families [24]. Marro et al. reformulate frontier mathematical benchmarking
as an adversarial generation–evaluation game and report stable rankings that correlate with external
capability measures [25]. These papers are close in spirit to our aims, but they remain essentially cross￾sectional: they diagnose benchmark quality or redesign benchmark construction at a time slice rather
than treating validity as a dynamical state variable with a half-life.
2.18 Soft contamination, saturation, and recursive knowledge ecologies
Spiesberger et al. show that semantic duplicates can confound benchmark gains even when exact matching
misses the overlap [18]. Akhtar et al. show that benchmark saturation is already widespread and that
benchmark age and test scale matter more than several common folk remedies [20]. Nettasinghe and Zhao
model the web as a human–AI collective-knowledge system with feedback loops, regime changes, and
quality dilution risks [21]. Salaudeen et al. show, in a neighboring benchmark domain, that apparently
robust benchmark behavior can be an artifact of misspecification rather than genuine measurement of
the intended phenomenon [26]. These works motivate four core choices of our theory: semantic leakage
must be broader than exact overlap, validity must be time-indexed, construct drift must be treated as a
first-class failure mode, and nominal data origin is no longer a stable primitive.
Gap statement. To our knowledge, the literature still lacks a unified state-variable theory that treats
benchmark validity as a dynamical quantity in recursive corpora, derives explicit lifetime bounds from
semantic leakage, evaluator coupling, construct drift, support erosion, and regeneration, makes explicit
which quantities are point identified, set identified, or not identified under noisy anchors, resolved-set
15

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
mismatch, incomplete provenance, imperfect contamination detectors, and aggregate-versus-factor hazard
observability, sharpens imperfect-exclusion analysis into explicit identified sets, extends lifetime reasoning
from single benchmarks to benchmark portfolios exposed to both idiosyncratic and common-mode decay
together with explicit design choices under scenario uncertainty, and connects those lifetime summaries to
safe sequential control of retirement and regeneration. This is the gap addressed here.
3 Mathematical Setup

3.2 Recursive corpora and semantic leakage
Let 𝒳 denote the space of publicly circulating artifacts that can influence future training, evaluation, or
benchmark-aware behavior: benchmark items, paraphrases, worked solutions, tutorials, model-generated
traces, discussion threads, retrieval targets, judge instructions, and so on.
Let 𝜇𝑡 be an exposure measure on 𝒳 representing the artifact mass available in the recursive corpus
environment at time 𝑡. For each benchmark item 𝑏 ∈ 𝐵, let 𝜅𝑏 : 𝒳 → [0, 1] be a semantic-neighborhood
kernel that measures how much an artifact 𝑥 leaks the evaluative content of 𝑏. Exact duplicates correspond
to sharp kernels; semantic duplicates, paraphrases, and worked variants correspond to broader kernels.
Definition 8 (Semantic leakage mass). The semantic leakage mass of benchmark 𝐵 at time 𝑡 is
𝑆𝐵(𝑡) := 1

When the benchmark is clear from context we write 𝑆𝑡
.
This definition is functional rather than ontological. Leakage depends on what an artifact reveals
about benchmark content, not on whether that artifact is labeled human, synthetic, or hybrid.

3.3 State variables
We separate the latent state into an ecological state, which governs passive validity decay, and an
observability state, which governs how sharply that ecology can be inferred and audited.
The ecological state is
𝑋eco
𝑡
:= (𝑆𝑡
, 𝑁𝑡
, 𝐹𝑡
, 𝑈𝑡
, 𝐽𝑡
, 𝐾𝑡). (16)
Its coordinates are benchmark-specific and have the following meanings:
𝑆𝑡
:= semantic leakage mass,
𝑁𝑡
:= verified novelty inflow,
𝐹𝑡
:= verification fidelity,
𝑈𝑡
:= support diversity / tail coverage,
𝐽𝑡
:= evaluator coupling,
𝐾𝑡
:= construct drift.
Their intended meanings are:
• 𝑆𝑡
increases when the recursive corpus contains benchmark items or semantically nearby artifacts that
make benchmark success easier for benchmark-specific rather than capability-general reasons.
• 𝑁𝑡 measures the inflow of items that are genuinely new relative to plausible exposure and that remain
verifiable enough to serve as evaluation material.
• 𝐹𝑡 measures answer verifiability and evaluator fidelity: objective ground truth, validator robustness,
and low judge fragility all increase 𝐹𝑡
.
• 𝑈𝑡 measures whether the benchmark still covers diverse or hard regions of the intended task distribution,
including tails rather than only central modes.
• 𝐽𝑡 measures coupling between the evaluated system and the evaluative apparatus, including shared
judge families, benchmark-generation overlap, style-matching effects, or common curation pipelines.
• 𝐾𝑡 measures drift between what the benchmark was designed to support claims about and what it now
effectively measures.
The observability state is

𝑡

𝑡

where 𝐿𝑡

summaries or validation-design records, and Λ
jump
𝑡
records the declared jump log. Its intended meanings
are:
• 𝐿𝑡 measures how well provenance, timestamps, revisions, or construction logs support auditing and
partial identification.
• 𝐻𝑡 stores calibration metadata, proxy-quality summaries, validation-study outputs, and other objects
used to construct identified sets and uncertainty budgets.
• Λ
jump
𝑡
records the declared intervention log needed to certify regime-time jump multipliers and their
envelopes.

𝑡

Low 𝐹𝑡 can directly harm validity because poor verification distorts both scores and claims. Low 𝐿𝑡 mainly
widens uncertainty because it obstructs provenance-aware auditing, weakens jump-factor certification,
and restricts the action set that can be justified safely. We therefore let 𝐹𝑡 enter the passive decay law
directly, while 𝐿𝑡 enters the identification, envelope-width, and control-admissibility results through the
observability state.
Proposition 9 (Observability affects feasible governance without changing the passive hazard law).

𝑡 and declared operational

𝑡 and (𝐴obs
𝑡

However, any identification region, jump-factor envelope, safe action set, or ambiguity set built from the
reported audit objects may differ across the two observability states. Thus observability need not enter the
passive hazard to matter operationally.
Proof. If the passive hazard depends only on 𝑋eco

remaining differences arise through the inferential objects built from auditability, calibration, and jump
certification, which are by construction functions of the observability state.
3.4 Modeling assumptions
Operational specifications, judge protocols, or benchmark contents need not evolve smoothly. We therefore
distinguish passive decay intervals from declared regime times. Let
0 = 𝜏0 < 𝜏1 < 𝜏2 < · · ·
be a locally finite sequence containing all declared benchmark revisions, operational-scope changes, judge
changes, or explicit regeneration actions. On each open interval (𝜏𝑘, 𝜏𝑘+1), the benchmark artifact and
operational specification are held fixed and validity evolves passively. At the regime times themselves, the
observed validity path is assumed càdlàg and may jump because the instrument has been changed rather
than because passive ecological pressure acted continuously. All envelope and hazard statements below
apply intervalwise on passive intervals, while half-life is always defined on the full càdlàg path.
Assumption 10 (Envelope-valid passive benchmark dynamics). On each passive interval (𝜏𝑘, 𝜏𝑘+1) on
which 𝒱𝐵(𝑡) > 0, the restriction of 𝒱𝐵 to that interval is absolutely continuous and there exist measurable
effective-rate envelopes 𝑞𝐵
, 𝑞𝐵 : [0,∞) → [0, ∞) such that for almost every 𝑡 in that interval,
−𝑞𝐵(𝑡) ≤

d𝑡
log 𝒱𝐵(𝑡) ≤ −𝑞𝐵
(𝑡). (19)
This weak assumption is sufficient for intervalwise envelope bounds, finite half-life upper bounds, and
fail-closed retirement rules. Jumps at declared regime times are handled separately by the càdlàg path
convention rather than forced into the hazard itself.

Assumption 16 (Coordinatewise monotonicity of factor rates). The factor-rate maps are coordinatewise

• 𝑞𝐷 is nondecreasing in 𝑆𝑡

Definition 17 (Exclusive and shared factor coordinates as optional design regimes). The baseline factor
law in theorem 15 allows every ecological coordinate to affect both factor hazards. An exclusive-coordinate
design regime is an additional substantive restriction under which a declared coordinate block affects one
factor hazard directly and the other factor hazard only through a known zero or bounded spillover channel.
The exact-zero special case is used only in the cleanest intervention-identification results. Bounded-spillover
variants are treated later as sensitivity classes rather than as background truths.
Definition 18 (Effective decay rate). Under theorem 13, the effective benchmark decay rate is
𝑞𝐵(𝑡) := 𝑞𝑉 (𝑋eco
𝑡
). (27)
Under theorem 15, one may additionally write
𝑞𝐵(𝑡) = 𝑞𝐷

)︀
+ 𝑞𝐶
(︀
𝑋eco
𝑡
)︀
(28)
on intervals where 𝒱𝐵(𝑡) > 0.
The assumption ladder is deliberate. The envelope assumption is weakest and is enough for fail-closed
retirement bounds and, when rate envelopes separate across ecologies, for cross-ecology ordering. The
state-reduced effective-rate law is stronger and is used for statewise comparison, proxy-based point
identification, conditional interval propagation, and regeneration arguments. The factor-specific exact law
is stronger still and is used only when one wants separate statements about discriminative power and
construct validity. Exclusive-coordinate restrictions are not part of the baseline law; they are optional
design-side assumptions that can be invoked, rejected, or weakened through explicit sensitivity budgets.

3.5 A useful linear proxy
For closed-form governance thresholds it is convenient to consider a linear proxy for the passive hazard,
𝑞
lin
𝐵 (𝑡) := [︁
𝑎𝑆𝑆𝑡 + 𝑎𝐽 𝐽𝑡 + 𝑎𝐾𝐾𝑡 + 𝑎𝐹 (1 − 𝐹𝑡) + 𝑎𝑈 (1 − 𝑈𝑡) − 𝑎𝑁 𝑁𝑡
]︁
+
, (29)
with nonnegative coefficients 𝑎∙ and [𝑥]+ := max{𝑥, 0}. The positive-part operator matters. Verified
novelty inflow can offset passive decay pressure down to zero, but passive hazard itself should not become
negative. Validity gains above the current level are modeled through declared regeneration or redesign
jumps at regime times, not by pretending that a negative decay rate is the same thing as maintenance.
The main comparison and interval results do not require linearity. The proxy is used only for explicit
thresholds and phase sketches.
4 Why Origin Labels Fail as Primitives
The usual temptation is to classify data by nominal origin: human-origin artifacts are presumed safer,
synthetic artifacts are presumed riskier. In recursive corpora this is not a stable primitive. A text may
be human-written with AI assistance, AI-generated and lightly edited by a human, or produced by a
human whose own cognitive state has been shaped by prior AI interaction. More importantly, even
when provenance is known, origin does not coincide with instrumental quality. High-novelty synthetic
content can improve downstream reasoning or help stabilize recursive training regimes, while low-novelty
human-origin content can still leak benchmark structure and erode validity [3, 22, 21].
We formalize this in an observational and decision-theoretic way.
Definition 19 (Latent production history). Let 𝒵 be a latent space of production histories. A history
𝑧 ∈ 𝒵 includes the causal path by which an artifact entered the recursive corpus, including any mixture of
human and AI intervention. Let 𝑜(𝑧) denote a nominal origin label, and let 𝒪 : 𝒵 → 𝒴 be the observation
map that retains only publicly available information relevant to governance.
Proposition 20 (Observation-level origin non-identifiability). Suppose there exist 𝑧, 𝑧′ ∈ 𝒵 such that
𝒪(𝑧) = 𝒪(𝑧
′
) but 𝑜(𝑧) ̸= 𝑜(𝑧
′
). (30)
Then no governance rule measurable with respect to the observed sigma-field can distinguish 𝑧 from 𝑧
′
by nominal origin. If, in addition, 𝑧 and 𝑧
′
induce the same declared operational specification, the same
ecological state trajectory 𝑋eco
𝑡
, and the same declared jump-multiplier path, then they are indistinguishable
with respect to passive validity trajectories, jump-accounted validity paths, and any other validity objects in
the present model that depend only on those quantities. They need not be indistinguishable with respect to
identified sets, jump-factor envelopes, or safe action sets unless the observability states also agree.
Proof. Any governance rule based only on observed information factors through 𝒪. If 𝒪(𝑧) = 𝒪(𝑧
′
), the
rule must take the same value on both histories. For the second claim, theorem 13 implies that on passive
intervals the validity path is determined by the declared operational specification and the ecological state
trajectory alone. The jump-accounting convention then multiplies those passive factors by the same
declared jump multipliers, so the resulting full càdlàg validity path agrees as well. The final sentence is
exactly the distinction formalized in theorem 9: observability affects inferential and control objects even
when it leaves the passive hazard law unchanged.
23

=== PDF PAGE 24 ===
Observation-level non-identifiability is only the first step. Even when an origin label is observed, it
can still be an insufficient statistic for governance.
Proposition 21 (Origin labels are not sufficient statistics). Suppose there exist two feasible recursive￾corpus histories 𝑧, 𝑧′ ∈ 𝒵 with the same nominal origin label process 𝑜(𝑧) = 𝑜(𝑧
′
) but distinct observed
state summaries. Let 𝒜 be a feasible action set for benchmark governance, and suppose there exist
actions 𝑎, 𝑎′ ∈ 𝒜 such that an origin-only policy must choose the same action on both histories, whereas a
state-based policy may choose 𝑎 on one history and 𝑎
′ on the other. Assume furthermore that the induced
post-action ecological state trajectories satisfy a shared passive hazard law with the same monotone map
𝑞𝑉 and satisfy the coordinatewise dominance inequalities later formalized in the statewise comparison
principle. Then origin-only governance cannot, in general, attain the best feasible validity trajectory on
both histories.
Proof. An origin-only rule cannot distinguish the two histories by assumption, so it must choose the same
feasible action on both. By construction, the state-based policy class can condition on the observed state
summaries and choose different feasible actions. Under the stated shared-hazard and coordinatewise￾dominance conditions, the usual monotone comparison argument implies that the state-based rule weakly
dominates the origin-only path on both histories and is strictly better on at least one whenever the
dominance is strict on a set of positive measure. Thus origin labels are not sufficient statistics for attainable
benchmark-governance quality.
Remark 22. These propositions are deliberately modest. They do not claim that provenance metadata
are useless. They claim only that nominal origin is neither reliably identifiable from observations nor
sufficient for validity governance once state-overlap and human–AI co-production are allowed.
5 Decay Dynamics and Benchmark Lifetime
5.1 Comparison principle and source-agnostic quality ordering
The weakest cross-ecology ordering result in the paper uses only rate envelopes. It does not require a
common state map.

Corollary 26 (Source-agnostic quality dominance). Suppose two candidate data-update policies or source
streams induce state trajectories satisfying the ordering in theorem 24. Then the lower-rate stream weakly
dominates the higher-rate stream in benchmark validity over time, regardless of nominal source label.
This is the formal version of “human versus synthetic is the wrong contrast.” The relevant contrast is
low-rate versus high-rate contribution to the recursive corpus.

5.2 Leakage monotonicity, finite half-life, and regeneration
The next result isolates semantic leakage.

This theorem formalizes the basic point that a static public benchmark exposed to persistent positive
decay pressure is a finite-lifetime instrument.
Corollary 32 (Certified no-redesign window). Under the setup of theorem 31, suppose governance declares
a certified no-redesign window [𝑡0, 𝑡1] on which no upward validity jump is admissible, either because all
interventions are prohibited or because every admissible intervention is guaranteed not to increase validity.
Then the half-life conclusion of theorem 31 is ex ante certifiable on that window.
This separates two use cases. Theorem 31 is an ex post passive-window theorem. Theorem 32 gives
the ex ante design rule needed when one wants a governance guarantee rather than a retrospective
decomposition.
Corollary 33 (Passive-regeneration threshold in the linear proxy). Assume the passive decay rate is
bounded above by the linear proxy (29). If for almost every 𝑡 ∈ [0, 𝑇],
𝑎𝑁 𝑁𝑡 ≥ 𝑎𝑆𝑆𝑡 + 𝑎𝐽 𝐽𝑡 + 𝑎𝐾𝐾𝑡 + 𝑎𝐹 (1 − 𝐹𝑡) + 𝑎𝑈 (1 − 𝑈𝑡), (48)
then 𝑞
lin
𝐵 (𝑡) = 0 on [0, 𝑇]. Consequently the passive component of validity does not decay on any passive
interval contained in [0, 𝑇], so any change in 𝒱𝐵 over that horizon must come from declared regime jumps
such as regeneration, scope restriction, or benchmark redesign. If the inequality holds with additional
slack 𝛿 > 0, then the same no-passive-decay conclusion still holds, and the slack can be interpreted as
regeneration headroom rather than as a negative passive hazard.
The interpretation is not that any churn prevents decay. The point is that verified regeneration must
be strong enough to cancel passive decay pressure from leakage, coupling, drift, poor verification, and
support erosion before any explicit redesign jump can improve the instrument.

5.4 Exact overlap versus semantic leakage
Exact matching is an attractive operational shortcut, but it is systematically optimistic whenever semantic
duplicates exist.

6 Identification, Proxies, and Factor Attribution
6.1 Partial identification and the detection–estimation gap
In practice, 𝑆𝑡
, 𝐽𝑡
, 𝐾𝑡
, and sometimes 𝑁𝑡 or 𝐹𝑡 are not observed exactly. Detectors disagree, provenance
is incomplete, and benchmark-aware fine-tuning can obscure exposure [7, 8, 9, 19]. The correct object
is therefore often an interval, not a point. More precisely, once a hazard map 𝑞𝑉 has been calibrated,
learned, or otherwise justified for the operational class of interest, bounded latent states propagate to
bounded validity trajectories. Before stating such propagation results, it is useful to mark the identification
boundary sharply.
Theorem 39 (Score-trace non-identifiability of state–hazard decomposition). Let 𝒱𝐵 : [0, 𝑇] → (0, ∞) be

6.2 Minimal calibration conditions for hazard-map identification
The interval results above are deliberately permissive: they allow broad calibration correspondences and
therefore broad validity envelopes. One can ask when the stronger object, the structural hazard map 𝑞𝑉 ,
becomes point identified rather than merely bounded.
Definition 45 (State-recovery regime). A state-recovery regime is any declared identification design that
point identifies the ecological path 𝑋eco
𝑡 on a horizon of interest before any attempt is made to identify the
hazard map itself. Examples include direct audit recovery, injective proxy inversion, calibrated latent-state
34

=== PDF PAGE 35 ===
recovery from repeated audits, or experimentally designed support excitation together with an externally
justified proxy-calibration map. Hazard-map identification in this paper is always conditional on belonging
to such a regime; it is not claimed to arise from score traces alone.
Definition 46 (Support-dense calibrated design). Fix a compact target set 𝐾 ⊆ 𝒮 and a horizon [0, 𝑇].
A calibration design is support-dense on 𝐾 if:
1. the ecological state 𝑋eco
𝑡
is point identified for almost every 𝑡 ∈ [0, 𝑇] from a declared state-recovery
regime;
2. the composite hazard ℎ(𝑡) := −
d
d𝑡
log 𝒱𝐵(𝑡) is identified for almost every 𝑡 ∈ [0, 𝑇]; and
3. the identified state image
ℐ𝑇 := {𝑋eco
𝑡
: 𝑡 ∈ [0, 𝑇] outside a null set}
is dense in 𝐾.
Theorem 47 (Hazard-map identification on calibrated support). Assume theorem 13. Let 𝐾 ⊆ 𝒮 be
compact, and suppose 𝑞𝑉 is continuous on 𝐾. If the calibration design is support-dense on 𝐾, then 𝑞𝑉 is
point identified on 𝐾.
Proof. By theorem 13, the identified hazard and identified state satisfy
ℎ(𝑡) = 𝑞𝑉 (𝑋eco
𝑡
) for almost every 𝑡 ∈ [0, 𝑇].
Hence the values of 𝑞𝑉 are identified on the dense set ℐ𝑇 ∩ 𝐾. Because 𝑞𝑉 is continuous on the compact
set 𝐾, there is at most one continuous extension from that dense set to all of 𝐾. Therefore 𝑞𝑉 is point
identified on 𝐾.
Proposition 48 (Off-support non-identifiability of continuous hazard maps). Assume theorem 13. Let
𝐾 ⊆ 𝒮 be compact and suppose the identified state image ℐ𝑇 is not dense in 𝐾. Then, in general, 𝑞𝑉
is not point identified on 𝐾 even if 𝑋eco
𝑡 and ℎ(𝑡) are point identified for almost every 𝑡 ∈ [0, 𝑇]. More
precisely, there exist distinct continuous maps 𝑞, 𝑞′
: 𝐾 → [0, ∞) that agree on ℐ𝑇 ∩ 𝐾 and therefore induce
the same observations on [0, 𝑇].
Proof. Because ℐ𝑇 is not dense in 𝐾, there exists a nonempty open set 𝑈 ⊆ 𝐾 disjoint from ℐ𝑇 . Choose
any nonnegative continuous bump function 𝜓 supported in 𝑈 and not identically zero. For any continuous
map 𝑞 consistent with the observations on ℐ𝑇 ∩ 𝐾, the perturbed map 𝑞
′
:= 𝑞 + 𝜓 is continuous, distinct
from 𝑞, and agrees with 𝑞 on ℐ𝑇 ∩ 𝐾. Since the observed state path never enters 𝑈, both maps induce the
same hazard values along the observed path and are observationally equivalent on [0, 𝑇].
Corollary 49 (Minimal calibration ingredients). Point identification of a hazard map on a target region
requires three ingredients: point-calibrated state recovery, identification of the composite hazard along the
observed path, and sufficiently rich support or experimental excitation to cover the target region. If any
one of these ingredients fails, the structural hazard map is generally only set identified.
The logical moral is slightly annoying but useful: one does not get a structural hazard map merely by
drawing a smooth curve through validity points. One gets it only on the portion of state space that is
first recovered by a declared state-recovery regime and then visited densely enough to pin the map down.
35

=== PDF PAGE 36 ===
6.3 Projected-support extrapolation under minimal additional restrictions
Theorem 48 shows that off-support point identification is impossible without extra structure. The next
results isolate weak additional restrictions that convert that impossibility into sharp set identification
rather than unjustified point extrapolation.
Definition 50 (Identified support and Lipschitz hazard class). Fix a compact target region 𝐾 ⊆ 𝒮 and

Taking the supremum and infimum over 𝑦 ∈ 𝐸 gives the displayed interval, together with nonnegativity.
For sharpness, fix 𝑣 ∈ 𝐼𝐿(𝑥) and define 𝑓𝑣 on 𝐸 ∪ {𝑥} by 𝑓𝑣|𝐸 = 𝑞𝐸 and 𝑓𝑣(𝑥) = 𝑣. The membership

so 𝑓𝑣 is 𝐿-Lipschitz on 𝐸 ∪ {𝑥}. By McShane’s extension theorem [41], there exists an 𝐿-Lipschitz map

Because 𝑧 ↦→ max{0, 𝑧} is 1-Lipschitz, 𝑞̃︀ is again 𝐿-Lipschitz and nonnegative. It agrees with 𝑓𝑣 on 𝐸 ∪ {𝑥}
because all values of 𝑓𝑣 are already nonnegative there. Hence 𝑞̃︀ ∈ 𝒬+
𝐿
(𝐾) is observationally admissible
and satisfies 𝑞̃︀(𝑥) = 𝑣.
Definition 52 (Validity-improving partial order). For 𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ 𝒮, write 𝑥 ⪯ 𝑦 if
𝑥𝑆 ≤ 𝑦𝑆, 𝑥𝑁 ≥ 𝑦𝑁 , 𝑥𝐹 ≥ 𝑦𝐹 , 𝑥𝑈 ≥ 𝑦𝑈 , 𝑥𝐽 ≤ 𝑦𝐽 , 𝑥𝐾 ≤ 𝑦𝐾.
Thus 𝑥 ⪯ 𝑦 means that 𝑦 is weakly better than 𝑥 in every coordinate according to theorem 14.
Proposition 53 (Sharp monotone extrapolation bracket). Assume theorems 13 and 14. Let 𝑥 ∈ 𝐾 and
define
𝐿⪯(𝑥) := sup{𝑞𝐸(𝑦) : 𝑦 ∈ 𝐸, 𝑦 ⪯ 𝑥}, 𝑈⪯(𝑥) := inf{𝑞𝐸(𝑦) : 𝑦 ∈ 𝐸, 𝑥 ⪯ 𝑦}, (79)
36

=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
with the conventions sup ∅ = 0 and inf ∅ = +∞. Then every admissible coordinatewise-monotone
extension of 𝑞𝐸 to 𝐾 satisfies
𝐿⪯(𝑥) ≤ 𝑞𝑉 (𝑥) ≤ 𝑈⪯(𝑥). (80)
Moreover, if 𝐿⪯(𝑥) ≤ 𝑈⪯(𝑥), then every value 𝑣 ∈ [𝐿⪯(𝑥), 𝑈⪯(𝑥)] is attained at 𝑥 by some nonnegative
coordinatewise-monotone extension on 𝐾 consistent with 𝑞𝐸 on 𝐸.
Proof. If 𝑦 ⪯ 𝑥, coordinatewise monotonicity gives 𝑞𝐸(𝑦) ≤ 𝑞𝑉 (𝑥); taking the supremum yields the lower
bound. The upper bound is analogous.
For sharpness, fix 𝑣 ∈ [𝐿⪯(𝑥), 𝑈⪯(𝑥)] and define 𝑓𝑣 on 𝐸 ∪ {𝑥} by 𝑓𝑣|𝐸 = 𝑞𝐸 and 𝑓𝑣(𝑥) = 𝑣. The
bracket condition guarantees that whenever 𝑦 ⪯ 𝑥 one has 𝑓𝑣(𝑦) ≤ 𝑓𝑣(𝑥), and whenever 𝑥 ⪯ 𝑦 one has
𝑓𝑣(𝑥) ≤ 𝑓𝑣(𝑦). Since 𝑞𝐸 itself is monotone on 𝐸, the map 𝑓𝑣 is monotone on the partially ordered set
𝐸 ∪ {𝑥}. Now define
𝑞̃︀(𝑧) := sup{𝑓𝑣(𝑦) : 𝑦 ∈ 𝐸 ∪ {𝑥}, 𝑦 ⪯ 𝑧}. (81)
This map is nonnegative and coordinatewise monotone by construction. If 𝑧 ∈ 𝐸 ∪ {𝑥}, monotonicity of
𝑓𝑣 implies that every 𝑦 ⪯ 𝑧 in the support satisfies 𝑓𝑣(𝑦) ≤ 𝑓𝑣(𝑧), while 𝑧 itself belongs to the admissible
set; hence 𝑞̃︀(𝑧) = 𝑓𝑣(𝑧). Therefore 𝑞̃︀ is a monotone extension consistent with the observed support and
attaining the prescribed value 𝑣 at 𝑥.
Corollary 54 (Minimal additional structure for off-support inference). Outside calibrated support,
point identification is impossible without additional structure. A known Lipschitz modulus or a justified
coordinatewise monotone order yields sharp set identification. If both are justified, the pointwise identified
set contracts to
𝐼𝐿(𝑥) ∩ [𝐿⪯(𝑥), 𝑈⪯(𝑥)]. (82)

The benchmark-validity hazard is an aggregate object. If one wants to know whether decay is arriving
primarily through loss of discriminative power or through loss of construct validity, the aggregate trace is
not enough by itself.

Corollary 58 (Minimal ingredients for factor-specific identification). Point identification of factor-specific
hazard maps requires more information than point identification of the aggregate hazard. In addition
to calibrated ecological-state recovery and support richness, one must separately observe or identify the
discriminative-power and construct-validity traces. Without those factor traces, only the composite hazard
is generally identified.

6.5 Factor-specific intervention design
Passive observation is not the only route to factor attribution. Because some state coordinates enter
only one factor hazard, matched interventions can sometimes recover factor-level effects even when only
aggregate validity is observed.
Definition 59 (Exclusive and shared factor coordinates). Under the baseline factor law, every ecological
coordinate may affect both factor hazards. A coordinate block is called 𝐷-exclusive only under a declared
design regime in which 𝑞𝐶 is invariant to that block along the intervention family under study. A
coordinate block is 𝐶-exclusive analogously. Coordinates not covered by such a restriction are shared. In
many applications (𝑆, 𝑈) are plausible candidates for approximate 𝐷-exclusive perturbations and 𝐾 for
approximate 𝐶-exclusive perturbations, but those claims are empirical design assumptions rather than
part of the ambient model.

map is differentiable, dividing the identified finite difference by the coordinate step and letting the step
size vanish yields the directional derivative.
6.6 Shared-coordinate decomposition under exclusion and IV-style designs
Shared coordinates remain harder than exclusive ones because changing 𝑁, 𝐹, or 𝐽 generally moves
both factor hazards at once. Still, design can sometimes restore identification. The key is to distinguish
passive shared-coordinate perturbations, which identify only sums, from designed perturbations that
satisfy exclusion restrictions or generate linearly independent local transport across the two factors.

Remark 67. The theorem is intentionally local. It does not pretend that arbitrary shared-coordinate
interventions magically reveal factor decomposition. What it says is narrower and more useful: if the
experimental protocol supplies either exclusion or two linearly independent local transport patterns across
the factors, then aggregate validity can support factor attribution even without full factor traces. Without
that design information, theorem 61 still rules.
6.7 Shared-coordinate sensitivity under imperfect exclusion and invalid IVs
Exact exclusion is a useful limiting case, not a default fact of life. A shared-coordinate intervention
that is intended to move only one factor may still weakly perturb the other through hidden pathways,
implementation spillovers, or judge adaptation. The right object is therefore often a sensitivity region
indexed by declared violation budgets, in the same spirit as plausibly exogenous IV analysis [47].
Definition 68 (Exclusion-violation budget). Fix a shared coordinate 𝑍 ∈ {𝑁, 𝐹, 𝐽}, a window [𝑡0, 𝑡1],

6.8 Structural sharpness under imperfect exclusion
Violation classes need not be loose boxes. Many designs imply additional structure across matched contrasts:
one-sided spillovers, monotone violation growth across exposure intensities, sparse contamination channels,
or linear coupling induced by shared implementation pathways. Those restrictions should narrow the
identified set transparently rather than being left as verbal folklore.

45

=== PDF PAGE 46 ===
If the inclusion of violation classes is strict and both classes are attainable, then the inclusion of sharp
identified sets is also strict.
Proof. Affine images preserve set inclusion. Strictness follows because a strictly smaller attainable violation
class omits at least one feasible nuisance vector whose image would otherwise appear in the larger identified
set.
Remark 80. This result is mathematically simple and operationally important. Once the violation
class carries real design structure—for example, one-sided spillovers, nondecreasing direct effects across
exposure levels, or a sparse shared-pathway model—the sharp region contracts by exactly the affine image
of that structure. The honest object is therefore not “a sensitivity interval” in the abstract, but the sharp
image of the declared structural violation class.
6.9 Construct-validity identification with noisy anchors
Construct validity is harder to estimate than discriminative power because the target-claim functional 𝐺𝑡
is typically latent or only partially externalized. In the present paper the construct-validity component is
𝑅𝐵(𝑡)𝒪𝐵(𝑡), so the identification problem has two parts: resolved-pair mass and order fidelity on those
resolved pairs.
Let

Definition 81 (Anchor-validation design). An anchor-validation design is an auxiliary study, model,
or calibration argument that targets the anchor error on the resolved-pair event by one of the following
observable routes:
1. a gold-standard adjudication design, in which a sampled subset of resolved pairs is labeled by a
higher-fidelity adjudication protocol;
2. a multi-anchor latent-label design, in which repeated imperfect anchors are fit under a latent-label
model and the resulting identified set is reported;
3. a one-sided calibration design, in which domain knowledge certifies that some disagreement modes are
impossible or bounded.
The design is admissible for theorem 83 on a declared analysis event if the resulting observable outputs
imply an upper bound 𝑒¯𝐴(𝑡) satisfying 𝑒𝐴(𝑡) ≤ 𝑒¯𝐴(𝑡) on that event.
Theorem 82 (Observable sufficient conditions for admissible anchor-error budgets). Fix time 𝑡 and the
resolved-pair event ℛ𝑡. Each of the following is sufficient to produce an admissible upper bound on 𝑒𝐴(𝑡).

Proof. For the adjudication design, on ℰref the reference labels equal the latent target order, so the

Remark 95. A symmetric-difference penalty is safe but blunt. Directional mismatch budgets expose
whether the observable anchor threshold is too strict, too loose, or both. In the nested cases the identified
set is not merely bounded more tightly; it is sharp.

Proof. Conditional on (𝑍𝑡 = 𝑧, ℛ𝑡), the number of panel errors is a sum of independent Bernoulli variables
with success probabilities 𝑒𝑟(𝑧, 𝑡). The conditional majority-error probability is therefore the stated

rate. The exchangeable formula is the binomial special case.
7 Benchmark Portfolios and Coverage Uncertainty
7.1 Benchmark portfolio half-life
Single-benchmark half-life is often too brittle for real evaluation programs, which usually deploy families of
benchmarks whose decay channels are only partly shared. This motivates a portfolio-level lifetime object.

7.2 Portfolio design under deterministic and scenario uncertainty
The portfolio definitions above describe a given benchmark family. A governance program must still
decide how to allocate attention, refresh budget, and reporting weight across candidate benchmarks.
Proposition 104 (Deterministic horizon-survival design degenerates to a single benchmark). Fix a
horizon 𝑇 > 0 and suppose each candidate benchmark 𝐵𝑖 has known normalized survival

or uncertainty are imposed.
Proof. The objective is linear in 𝑤 over the simplex Δ𝑚. A linear objective on a simplex is maximized at
an extreme point, and the extreme points are precisely the unit vectors. Any unit vector supported on an
index with maximal 𝜎𝑖(𝑇) is therefore optimal.
Remark 105. This degeneracy is not a criticism of diversification; it clarifies why diversification needs a
reason. In practice that reason is usually one of four things: scenario uncertainty about survival profiles,
common-mode exposure caps, explicit coverage requirements, or risk aversion to tail invalidity rather than
mean survival. The later portfolio results make those motives explicit rather than treating diversification
as self-justifying.

Remark 108. Theorems 104 and 107 explain a small but important conceptual point. Benchmark
diversification is not automatically a theorem of good governance. It becomes rational only after one
acknowledges scenario uncertainty, common-mode concentration limits, or explicit coverage constraints.
Otherwise the optimal horizon-survival rule is embarrassingly greedy: use the single benchmark with the
best known survival profile at the target horizon.
7.3 Portfolio design with explicit coverage constraints
Robust survival is not the only design objective. A benchmark family may also be required to cover multiple
task types, modalities, demographic slices, attack surfaces, or capability regimes. Those requirements can
be formalized without breaking convexity.

Proposition 111 (Coverage constraints can force diversification). Assume the feasible set in theorem 109
is nonempty. If no single benchmark 𝑖 satisfies both the exposure caps and the coverage requirements

coverage constraints can break the single-benchmark degeneracy from theorem 104 even under a deterministic

Proof. A unit vector 𝑒𝑖 corresponds to a single-benchmark portfolio. By assumption, no such 𝑒𝑖

Remark 112. The coverage frontier 𝑣(𝜌; 𝑇) is a governance object, not just an optimization artifact. Its
shadow prices quantify how much worst-case horizon survival must be sacrificed to maintain representational
breadth across declared evaluation cells. That tradeoff should be reported whenever benchmark-family
design claims to balance longevity against scope.
Remark 113 (Computational scale). For fixed horizon 𝑇, the deterministic and scenario-robust portfolio
programs remain linear in the number of benchmarks, scenarios, and coverage cells. A direct LP formulation
has 𝑂(𝑚 + 𝑆 + 𝐺) primal variables before robustification and adds only one block of dual variables per
uncertain coverage cell in the box or budgeted models. When (𝑚, 𝐺, 𝑆) are large, one should work with
frontier slices, weighted-sum support-hyperplane methods, or column-generation style pruning rather than
enumerate the entire high-dimensional coverage frontier.

a frontier-attaining portfolio for each feasible 𝜌, while control sufficiency says no other portfolio feature
matters beyond Ψ(𝑤). Therefore optimization over portfolios can be replaced by optimization over feasible
coverage targets and their frontier values without loss relative to the certified action class.
Remark 116. This reduction is operationally useful because portfolio weights are often high dimensional
while the coverage target is low dimensional and policy relevant. The reduction is only as honest as the
certified lower frontier itself; if the frontier is learned with uncertainty, that uncertainty must be carried
into control rather than buried in a point frontier.
7.4 Coverage-matrix uncertainty
Coverage coefficients are rarely literal facts. They are usually outputs of audits, taxonomies, or evaluator
judgments, and those objects are themselves uncertain. Treating the coverage matrix as exact can therefore
produce fake diversification.

theorem 109 by Cov𝑔

7.5 Learned uncertainty sets for coverage matrices
Fixed box or budget sets are useful when governance wants explicit worst-case knobs. When coverage
claims are themselves estimated from audits, classifier outputs, or rater studies, however, the uncertainty
set should ideally be learned from data rather than chosen by hand. This is the benchmark-governance
analog of data-driven robust optimization [48].
Definition 121 (Learned coverage uncertainty set). Let 𝒟𝑛 denote audit data used to estimate the
unknown true coverage matrix 𝜅
⋆ ∈ [0, 1]𝑚×𝐺. A random set-valued map 𝒰̂︀
𝜅,1−𝛿(𝒟𝑛) is called a (1 − 𝛿)

P

𝜅
⋆ ∈ 𝒰̂︀
𝜅,1−𝛿(𝒟𝑛)
)︀
≥ 1 − 𝛿. (209)
The learned robust coverage design problem replaces the fixed set 𝒰𝜅 in theorem 117 by 𝒰̂︀
𝜅,1−𝛿(𝒟𝑛).

Proof. For fixed 𝑔 and 𝑤, the inner optimization is a linear program in 𝜅. Strong duality for nonempty

Remark 124. The theorem gives a transfer guarantee, not a fairy tale. If the learned set has poor
finite-sample coverage, or if the audit data are not representative of the taxonomy actually used in
deployment, the resulting robust portfolio can still be epistemically crooked. The logic is conditional on
honest uncertainty-set construction.

cluster-robust calibration, martingale methods, or other dependence-aware constructions. What matters

Proof. Condition on the realized audit history. The inner minimization over 𝜅 is then a deterministic

linear inequalities in the portfolio weights and dual variables. This argument applies pathwise at every
deterministic epoch and therefore also at any stopping time.
Remark 128. The point of the dependence-aware extension is plain: audit logs are often clustered by
benchmark family, evaluator, time window, or provenance chain. Treating them as i.i.d. fairy dust is
unnecessary and often wrong. What the downstream theory needs is not independence but a set sequence
whose coverage claim already respects the dependence actually present.
7.6 Dependent audits under mixing and martingale regimes
The previous subsection treated dependence-aware learned sets abstractly through their uniform
containment property. We now give two broad sufficient audit regimes under which that property
can be instantiated without pretending the audit rows are independent.

Remark 131. The theorem is deliberately modular. Under geometric mixing one may use blockwise
confidence sequences whose validity depends on the declared decay rate of the mixing coefficients, while
under the martingale regime one may use Freedman-style or nonnegative-supermartingale confidence
sequences driven by the predictable variance process [51, 52, 49]. The theory here does not privilege one
construction; it only insists that the downstream portfolio guarantee inherit whatever dependence-aware
validity the chosen construction actually has.

Proof. The claim is immediate because theorem 130 verifies the containment property required by
theorem 126.
7.7 Nonstationary and regime-switching audits
The previous subsection covered stationary mixing and martingale regimes. Real audit programs can also
be nonstationary because benchmark taxonomies, evaluator pools, provenance channels, or benchmark
families move between distinct regimes. The safe object is then a time-indexed or regime-indexed
uncertainty set rather than a single stationary one.

portfolio constraints, and the probability statement follows directly.
Remark 136. This result is intentionally fail-closed. It does not claim that arbitrary adversarial drift is
harmless. It says only that if one declares either observable regimes or a posterior-weighted regime model
and constructs regime-specific confidence sequences honestly, then the downstream coverage guarantee
can track that nonstationarity without pretending the audit world is stationary.

8 Sequential Monitoring and Safe Control
8.1 Time-to-invalidity beyond half-life
Half-life is useful, but it hard-codes the threshold 𝛼 = 1/2 and ignores the distributional shape of failure
times under scenario uncertainty. Operational governance often cares instead about the first time a
benchmark or portfolio becomes invalid relative to a task-specific threshold.

Remark 140. The point of these risk measures is not to make finance cosplay out of benchmark
governance. It is to stop pretending that one threshold at one confidence level tells the whole story. A
benchmark family can have the same half-life as another while exhibiting far worse lower-tail invalidity
risk at stricter thresholds or longer horizons.
8.2 Sequential monitoring and stopping rules
Static lifetime summaries are not enough once benchmark governance becomes a live process. Audits,
anchors, and contamination evidence arrive over time, and people will peek whenever they feel like it.
The correct safety object is therefore an anytime-valid envelope rather than a one-shot interval [49, 50].
Definition 141 (Anytime-valid validity envelope). Let 𝑍 denote either a single benchmark 𝐵 or a
benchmark portfolio 𝒫𝑤, and fix a thresholded validity object 𝑉̃︀

Proposition 144 (Safe continuation by lower-envelope certification). Under the same setup, let 𝜎 be any

Definition 145 (Operational life-cycle status). Fix a governance threshold 𝛼 ∈ (0, 1) and an anytime-valid
envelope (𝑉 𝑛, 𝑉 𝑛). At monitoring epoch 𝑛, classify the current benchmark or portfolio as
• active if 𝑉 𝑛 > 𝛼,
• watchlist if 𝑉 𝑛 ≤ 𝛼 < 𝑉 𝑛,
• regenerate-or-replace if 𝑉 𝑛 ≤ 𝛼 and at least one admissible repair action exists,
• retire-or-scope-restrict if 𝑉 𝑛 ≤ 𝛼 and no admissible repair action preserves the current operational
class.
The last category includes frontier-only failure cases in which the benchmark should remain alive only
after an explicit restriction of the operational class.
Remark 146. These statements are intentionally generic. The paper does not derive a universal confidence
sequence for benchmark validity from raw samples because that depends on the estimator class and
dependence structure. What it does show is how any valid online envelope can be converted into governance
actions without being invalidated by continuous monitoring or optional stopping.
8.3 Sequential retirement, regeneration, and safe dynamic control
Sequential monitoring becomes operational only when it is coupled to actions. A live benchmark program
may continue deploying the current instrument, regenerate or replace it, or retire it outright. The decision
problem is sequential and partially observed, but the safety constraint is simple: deployment decisions
must respect the anytime-valid envelope rather than a convenient point estimate.
Definition 147 (Envelope-admissible policy). Fix a governance threshold 𝛼 ∈ (0, 1) and monitoring
epochs 𝑛 = 0, 1, . . . , 𝐻 − 1. Let 𝐼𝑛 be any 𝒢𝑛-measurable information state that includes the current
anytime-valid envelope bounds (𝑉 𝑛, 𝑉 𝑛) for the deployed benchmark or benchmark portfolio, and let
70

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
ℛ(𝐼𝑛) denote the feasible regeneration or replacement actions at epoch 𝑛. A policy 𝜋 is called envelope
admissible if it satisfies two rules at every epoch:
1. it deploys the current benchmark for another epoch only when 𝑉 𝑛 > 𝛼;
2. whenever 𝑉 𝑛 ≤ 𝛼, it does not carry the current benchmark forward without either retiring it or
applying a regeneration/replacement action before any further deployment.
A regeneration or replacement action is not counted as continued operational use of the current benchmark;
it changes the instrument before the next deployment epoch.
Theorem 148 (Envelope-admissible policies are 𝛿-safe). Suppose (𝑉 𝑛, 𝑉 𝑛)
𝐻−1

𝑛 ≤ 𝛼
)︁
≤ 𝛿. (246)
If the deployed benchmark’s validity is nonincreasing between regeneration or replacement actions, then
on the envelope-coverage event no envelope-admissible policy retains the current benchmark past its true
invalidity time without either retiring it or replacing it.
Proof. On the envelope-coverage event, 𝑉 𝑛 ≤ 𝑉̃︀
𝑛 ≤ 𝑉 𝑛 holds for every epoch 𝑛. If the policy deploys
the current benchmark at epoch 𝑛, envelope admissibility requires 𝑉 𝑛 > 𝛼, which on the coverage event
implies 𝑉̃︀
𝑛 > 𝛼. Hence unsafe deployment can occur only if the coverage event fails, which has probability
at most 𝛿. For the second claim, if validity is nonincreasing between regeneration or replacement actions
and 𝑉 𝑛 ≤ 𝛼, then on the coverage event the current benchmark is already invalid at epoch 𝑛, so envelope
admissibility forbids carrying that instrument forward without retirement or replacement.

8.4 Safe control under partial observability
The previous control results treated the information state as if it were already the correct control state.
In practice the underlying benchmark ecology is only partially observed through audits, anchor outcomes,
contamination diagnostics, and regeneration logs. The natural control object is therefore a belief state.

1. Exact-belief convention. Under a declared generative model, define the Bayesian belief state

model separately at each reachable control-state/action pair, so the robust principle of optimality applies
and backward induction yields the displayed recursion.
Proposition 161 (Safety and reward optimality separate under partial observability). In the partially
observed setting, the deployment-safety guarantee from theorem 158 requires only that the lower-validity
sequence remain anytime valid. Robust value optimality claims, however, additionally require that the true
75

=== PDF PAGE 76 ===
joint transition–observation law belong to the reported ambiguity sets used by the planner. Hence model
misspecification can invalidate reward guarantees without necessarily invalidating the safety certificate.
Proof. Theorem 158 depends only on the implication that deployment occurs only when the reported lower￾validity quantity exceeds the governance threshold on the coverage event. The robust value statements
from theorem 160, by contrast, are defined relative to the ambiguity set itself; if the true law falls outside
that set, the pessimistic value comparison need not hold.
Remark 162. Rectangularity is not bookkeeping trivia. Without a stagewise rectangular ambiguity
structure, the exact stagewise Bellman separation can fail, and the honest object is a nested minimax or
scenario-tree control problem rather than a one-line recursion.
Definition 163 (Rectangular outer hull of a nonrectangular ambiguity family). Let Q̃︀ be any declared

stagewise rectangular family Rect(Q̃︀ ) such that for every (𝑛, 𝑍, 𝑎) with 𝑍 ∈ 𝒟𝑛, every one-step conditional
kernel generated by some law in Q̃︀ from (𝑛, 𝑍, 𝑎) belongs to the corresponding stagewise set in Rect(Q̃︀ ).

𝑛
(𝑍) for every control state 𝑍 that is reachable under some law in Q̃︀ at epoch 𝑛.
(272)
Hence rectangular-hull planning is a conservative lower-value surrogate even when exact rectangularity
is not justified. The explicit validity-based safety filter remains unchanged because it depends on the
lower-validity envelope rather than on dynamic rectangularity.
Proof. Every admissible law in Q̃︀ uses one-step conditional kernels that, by construction, belong to the
stagewise sets in Rect(Q̃︀ ) on the declared domain (𝒟𝑛). The rectangular outer hull therefore permits
every adversarial law in Q̃︀ and possibly additional history-inconsistent combinations of one-step kernels.
Taking an infimum over the larger rectangular class can only decrease the value. Applying theorem 160 to
the outer hull yields the displayed lower bound on the original-family reachable states. Safety follows
from the same envelope-admissibility logic as before and does not require equality of the two values.
Remark 165. The point of the rectangular hull is not metaphysical purity. It is to separate two claims
that are often sloppily fused: exact dynamic programming requires rectangularity, whereas conservative
planning only needs a reported outer hull. The latter is weaker, less elegant, and often more honest.
Proposition 166 (Rectangular-hull pessimism gap under one-step mismatch budgets). Under the setup
of theorem 164, suppose that for each epoch 𝑛 there exists 𝜖𝑛 ≥ 0 such that for every reachable control

Proof. Apply backward induction. At the terminal stage the values agree. If the one-step pessimism gap

at most 𝜖𝑛 plus the next-stage gap. Summing the recursion gives the bound.
8.5 Safe exploration–exploitation for regeneration policy
Learning which regeneration actions work best is itself a sequential problem. Exploration is useful because
hazard responses and regeneration effects are rarely known exactly, but exploration without safety control
is just a polite word for breaking the instrument on purpose.
Definition 167 (Optimistic safe regeneration on a finite abstraction). Fix an episodic horizon 𝐻 and a

operational moral needed here: once regeneration is learned online, one should report not just that the
policy is “adaptive” but the confidence system that keeps exploration inside the safe action set, together
with either an episodically frozen-dynamics justification or an explicit drift budget D𝐾 for nonstationarity.
8.6 Approximate safe dynamic programming and regret bounds
Exact safe dynamic programming can still be expensive once the information state includes uncertainty
envelopes, audit summaries, and portfolio structure. The next results show that approximate solvers
can be analyzed without sacrificing the explicit safety layer, provided one reports approximation error or
Bellman residuals honestly.

Remark 174. The Bellman residual 𝜂𝑛 is a sup-norm object over reachable safe states. In continuous

obtained from holdout grids, Lipschitz envelopes, certified function-approximation bounds, or reachability￾restricted sampling certificates. The theory does not require exact computation; it requires an honest
upper certificate.
