Remark 176. The theorem is intentionally local to reachable states with an interior safety margin. It
does not claim that the global safe value function must be Lipschitz across safety-boundary kinks where
the admissible action set changes discontinuously. The role of the margin condition 𝐿𝑉 ℎ < 𝜁𝑛 is precisely
to keep the projected recursion inside a layer on which the safe action set is locally stable.
8.7 From estimation to control: a joint certificate
The practical handoff from measurement to action is a common place for hidden wishful thinking. A
benchmark program estimates validity, estimates transitions, solves a planner, and then quietly acts as
if the whole chain inherited one undifferentiated truth value. It does not. The handoff itself needs a
certificate.
Definition 177 (Estimation-to-control certificate). Fix either the information-state control setup from
theorem 151 or the belief-state control setup from theorem 159. An estimation-to-control certificate at
horizon 𝐻 consists of
1. an anytime-valid validity envelope with failure budget 𝛿𝑉 ,
2. reported transition or joint transition–observation ambiguity sets with coverage budget 𝛿𝑃 ,
3. if portfolio redesign is part of the action space, a certified lower coverage frontier or certified lower
coverage/survival summaries with failure budget 𝛿𝐶,
4. a computed safe policy 𝜋̂︀ together with an optimization or approximation certificate 𝜀plan ≥ 0 for the
reported robust control problem.
81

=== PDF PAGE 82 ===
The certificate is called jointly valid if the true validity process lies in the reported envelope on an event
ℰ𝑉 with probability at least 1 − 𝛿𝑉 , the true controlled kernel law lies in the reported ambiguity sets on
an event ℰ𝑃 with probability at least 1 − 𝛿𝑃 , and any reported coverage frontier or certified portfolio
summaries are valid on an event ℰ𝐶 with probability at least 1 − 𝛿𝐶.
Theorem 178 (Joint estimation-to-control guarantee). Consider a policy 𝜋̂︀ produced from a jointly valid
certificate in the sense of theorem 177. Suppose 𝜋̂︀ is envelope admissible and is 𝜀plan-optimal for the
reported robust control problem over the certified action class. Then:
1. with probability at least 1 − 𝛿𝑉 , the policy never deploys the current benchmark at an epoch where the
true deployed validity is at or below the governance threshold 𝛼;
2. on the event ℰ𝑉 ∩ ℰ𝑃 , the expected value of 𝜋̂︀ under the true kernel law is at least the reported robust
value minus 𝜀plan;
3. on the event ℰ𝐶, any action pruning justified by theorem 115 is lossless relative to the certified action
class.
Consequently, the safety claim, the pessimistic expected-value claim, and the frontier-pruning claim hold
jointly with probability at least 1 − (𝛿𝑉 + 𝛿𝑃 + 𝛿𝐶).
Proof. The first claim is immediate from envelope admissibility and the anytime-validity event ℰ𝑉 , exactly
as in theorems 148 and 158. For the second claim, on ℰ𝑃 the true kernel law is one admissible element of
the ambiguity set used to compute the reported robust value. Therefore, for any policy 𝜋, its robust value
is no larger than its expected value under the true law on that event. Since 𝜋̂︀ is 𝜀plan-optimal for the
reported robust problem, its expected true value on ℰ𝑃 is at least the reported optimal robust value minus
𝜀plan. For the third claim, theorem 115 is deterministic conditional on the correctness of the reported
certified lower frontier or certified lower portfolio summaries, so it holds on ℰ𝐶. Intersecting the three
events and applying the union bound gives the stated joint probability.
Corollary 179 (Modular union-bound certification). If the validity envelope, ambiguity set, cover￾age/frontier certificate, and planner approximation certificate are produced by separate procedures with
failure budgets 𝛿𝑉 , 𝛿𝑃 , 𝛿𝐶, and 𝛿plan, then all reported claims hold simultaneously with probability at least
1 − (𝛿𝑉 + 𝛿𝑃 + 𝛿𝐶 + 𝛿plan). (303)
Proof. Apply the union bound to the four failure events.
9 Estimation Protocol
The theory becomes operational only if both the validity components and the state variables can be
estimated or bounded. This section sketches a practical protocol rather than a single mandated estimator.
9.1 Identification regimes before estimation
Before computing any estimate, one should classify the benchmark into an identification regime. If one
observes only the validity trace or score trace, then theorem 39 implies that only the composite hazard
path is identified; latent ecological coordinates are not. If proxies are available through a known injective
82

=== PDF PAGE 83 ===
map, then theorem 41 yields point identification of the ecological state and hazard along the observed
path. If, in addition, the protocol belongs to a declared state-recovery regime and attains a support-dense
calibrated design in the sense of theorems 47 and 49, then the structural aggregate hazard map is identified
on the visited target region; if not, off-support values remain non-identified by theorem 48. If one is
willing to impose only weak shape restrictions outside support, then theorems 51, 53 and 54 replace point
extrapolation by sharp set-valued extrapolation. If one separately observes the discriminative-power and
construct-validity traces, then theorems 55, 57 and 58 determine whether factor-specific hazards are non￾identified, point identified on calibrated support, or merely bounded. If only a calibration correspondence
is available, then the benchmark is set identified and the correct outputs are intervals as in theorems 42
and 43. If matched interventions on benchmark construction or exposure are feasible, then theorems 27,
60 to 62 and 64 to 66 determine whether one can recover only a sign, an exclusive-regime factor effect, an
exclusion-based shared-coordinate effect, or a full local shared-coordinate decomposition. At the portfolio
level, one should also classify whether coverage coefficients are treated as exact, box uncertain, or budget
uncertain in the sense of theorems 117 to 119, and whether the reporting target is a half-life, a threshold
invalidity time, or a tail-risk metric from Definition 137. This is a partial-identification mindset in the
sense of Manski-style analysis: the inferential target should match the information actually available
[31, 32, 37].
9.2 Calibrating the hazard map
A hazard map should not be treated as a freely chosen smooth summary. To justify it as a structural
object rather than a curve-fitting convenience, the empirical protocol should separately document three
ingredients from theorem 49: point-calibrated state recovery, identification or sufficiently tight smoothing
of the composite hazard path, and support-rich coverage of the target state region. In practice this means
combining proxy inversion or calibration studies for (𝑆𝑡
, 𝑁𝑡
, 𝐹𝑡
, 𝑈𝑡
, 𝐽𝑡
, 𝐾𝑡) with smoothed estimates of
−
d
d𝑡
log 𝒱𝐵(𝑡) and, when possible, deliberately varied benchmark-construction or exposure conditions that
excite the relevant coordinates.
The key caution is off-support extrapolation. Even a beautifully estimated hazard surface along observed
states does not identify what the surface would have been in unvisited regions of state space; theorem 48
shows that continuous alternatives can agree perfectly on the observed path and still diverge elsewhere.
Weak shape restrictions can tighten the admissible set without creating magic point identification:
theorems 51 and 53 yield sharp brackets, not warrant for unsupported certainty. Hence any reported
hazard map should explicitly distinguish identified-on-support regions from structurally extrapolated regions
and should name the restriction class used to produce the latter.
9.3 Designing factor-targeted interventions
If the empirical goal is factor attribution rather than passive monitoring, intervention design should
distinguish baseline factor laws from optional exclusivity regimes. Perturbations of 𝑆 or 𝑈 may isolate
discriminative-power effects in aggregate validity only when the design justifies treating the induced path
as approximately 𝐷-exclusive in the sense of theorem 60; perturbations of 𝐾 may isolate construct-validity
effects only under an analogous 𝐶-exclusive justification. Interventions on shared coordinates (𝑁, 𝐹, 𝐽)
are still useful, but aggregate validity then identifies only a sum of factor responses by theorem 61. To
recover a factor decomposition for shared coordinates, the protocol must either measure factor traces
83

=== PDF PAGE 84 ===
separately, impose a factor-excluding design as in theorem 64, or build at least two linearly independent
local perturbation families as in theorem 66.
A practical design rule is therefore simple: use exclusive-regime interventions only when their zero- or
bounded-spillover justification is written down, use exclusion-style shared-coordinate interventions when
one can justify them substantively, and use general shared-coordinate interventions for stress-testing. The
first class can pin down which factor moves, the second can recover local shared-coordinate sensitivities, and
the third reveals where the factor split remains underdetermined and therefore where extra instrumentation
is needed. Any claimed exclusion should be written down explicitly rather than implied by vibes.
9.4 Sensitivity analysis for imperfect exclusion
When shared-coordinate attribution relies on exclusion or IV-style logic, the protocol should never jump
directly from a design story to a point estimate. First declare the intended exclusion, then declare an
admissible violation budget or direct-effect set, and only then compute the induced identification region
using theorems 69, 72, 74 and 76. In practice this means writing down which implementation channels
could leak into the nominally excluded factor, assigning either deterministic bounds or statistically learned
bounds to those channels, and reporting how the factor estimate widens as those budgets increase. If the
conclusion changes under small plausible budgets, the design is fragile and should be reported as such
rather than laundered into a single coefficient.
9.5 Constructing sharp violation classes
A violation budget should be treated as a model of the admissible spillover class, not as a cosmetic
sensitivity knob. Whenever domain knowledge supports sign restrictions, asymmetric bounds, or a finite￾dimensional remainder polytope, the appropriate output is the sharp image set from theorems 72 and 76
rather than the looser symmetric interval from theorem 69. Operationally, this means documenting which
spillover paths are genuinely believed possible and which are ruled out by the intervention mechanics.

85

=== PDF PAGE 86 ===
these are not obtained for free from raw anchor agreement alone. Under the nondifferential-error condition
in theorem 84, theorem 85 then converts those inputs into either a point estimate or a valid identified set
for 𝒪𝐵(𝑡). If benchmark-dependent anchor error is plausible, the protocol should additionally declare a
violation budget of the form in theorem 87 and widen the estimate using theorem 88. Whenever only
intervals for the score-resolved inputs are available, theorem 86 should be used to push those intervals
through the affine recovery map rather than pretending that the latent prevalences are observed exactly.
When several imperfect anchors are available, the default nonparametric procedure is to construct
one order-fidelity interval from each anchor and then intersect them as in theorem 96. This already
tightens identification without assuming independence or any specific latent-noise model. An odd-sized
majority-vote panel provides a stronger but more model-dependent route: estimate the panel agreement
Γ̂︀maj(𝑡) and then combine it with an explicit panel-error bound, for example the shared-blind-spot bound
from theorem 98, the covariance-based bound from theorem 99, or the latent-factor mixture formula from
theorem 100. In practice, both an optimistic conditional-independence benchmark and a dependence￾robust benchmark should be reported. More structured alternatives include weighted panels, latent-label
models, or expert-adjudicated anchor hierarchies in the spirit of Dawid–Skene style observer-error modeling
[38]; the theory here only requires that the resulting anchor system come with a defensible order-error,
resolved-mass, and dependence budget.
Cross-benchmark corroboration metrics such as Benchmark2
’s ranking consistency can be useful
auxiliary diagnostics [24], but they are not by themselves construct validity, because peer benchmarks
can share the same blind spots. If no claim-aligned anchor exists, broad construct-validity claims should
be weakened or withheld. This is precisely where many benchmark papers overclaim relative to their
evidence [14, 16].
9.8 Qualified anchors and abstention budgets
In many real audits an anchor is only trusted on a visibly qualified subset of resolved pairs. The protocol
should therefore record the qualification rule 𝑄𝑡
, the lower qualification mass 𝜅𝐴(𝑡), and the qualified￾region error budget 𝑒¯𝐴,𝑄(𝑡), then apply theorem 90 rather than imputing accuracy on abstained pairs.
If no auditable lower bound on qualification mass is available, the abstention region must be treated
as unconstrained and the resulting order-fidelity interval will be correspondingly wide. Zero qualified
resolved pairs should be reported as insufficient-resolved-pairs, not converted into a point estimate
by denominator patching.
9.9 Estimating semantic leakage
For each benchmark item 𝑏, estimate 𝜅𝑏(𝑥) using a stack that combines exact matching, lexical overlap,
embedding-based semantic retrieval, template similarity, and solution-structure similarity. The goal is not
to decide whether 𝑥 is “the same item” in some binary metaphysical sense, but to assign leakage mass. A
sampled estimator takes the form
𝑆̂︀
𝑡 =
1
|𝐵|

calibration set for 𝑆𝑡
, not as an automatically identified latent state. At minimum it should be reported
with an uncertainty envelope rather than as a lone point estimate [18, 19].
9.10 Estimating verified novelty inflow
Novelty inflow is not mere timestamp churn. A new item contributes to 𝑁𝑡 only if it is temporally or
procedurally new relative to plausible exposure, materially distinct from prior benchmark manifolds, and
verifiable enough to support evaluation. A useful decomposition is
𝑁𝑡 = Λ𝑡
· 𝐸𝑡
· 𝑄𝑡
, (312)
where Λ𝑡
is a raw arrival rate of candidate items, 𝐸𝑡
is an effective novelty factor, and 𝑄𝑡
is a verifiability
gate. Dynamic benchmark systems can be read as attempts to raise Λ𝑡 and 𝐸𝑡 while keeping 𝑄𝑡 acceptably
high [10, 11, 13].
9.11 Estimating verification fidelity, support diversity, and coupling
Verification fidelity 𝐹𝑡 can be estimated from objective-answer availability, validator agreement, adversarial
stress tests, judge substitution sensitivity, and label-audit quality. Reliability-focused work on platinum
benchmarks is particularly relevant here because hidden label errors directly lower 𝐹𝑡 even when the
benchmark item format itself looks sound [23]. Support diversity 𝑈𝑡 can be estimated from coverage
metrics over benchmark subdomains, tails, difficulty strata, or perturbation families. Evaluator coupling 𝐽𝑡
can be proxied by shared judge families, overlap between benchmark-generation and evaluation pipelines,
or benchmark-specific prompt conventions. None of these proxies is perfect, which is precisely why interval
methods are more faithful than point claims.
9.12 Estimating construct drift
Construct drift 𝐾𝑡 measures misalignment between benchmark behavior and the target claim functional.
Indicators include weak transfer from benchmark gains to deployment outcomes, instability under small
task reframings, persistent “accuracy-on-the-line” style misspecification when the benchmark is meant to
test robustness, and evidence that scores are being driven by benchmark-specific exploitability rather than
the intended capability [14, 16, 15, 26]. Adversarial or expert-generated live benchmarks that correlate
with external capability measures can serve as drift audits rather than as replacements for the original
benchmark [25]. The formal recommendation is to build a construct-drift audit map
Ψ𝐾(transfer audits,reframing audits, external validity checks) ⇝ [𝐾𝑡
, 𝐾𝑡
], (313)
and to carry 𝐾𝑡 as an interval-valued ecological coordinate unless a stronger calibration study is available.
This keeps drift on the same inferential footing as leakage and coupling rather than leaving it as a verbal
warning.
9.13 Lineage observability and interval tightening
Lineage observability 𝐿𝑡
is estimated from timestamp coverage, source-chain completeness, revision logging,
benchmark-construction disclosure, and, when available, cryptographically anchored provenance. Within
87

=== PDF PAGE 88 ===
the present theory, stronger lineage observability does not automatically make a benchmark better; it
makes the benchmark’s life-cycle state more identifiable and can enlarge the set of actions that can be
justified safely. This is why 𝐿𝑡 enters the observability state and narrows the feasible set in theorems 9,
42 and 44 rather than appearing directly in the base decay rate.
9.14 Portfolio monitoring
Real evaluation programs often track a family of benchmarks rather than one instrument. For such
programs, the natural object to report is the weighted normalized portfolio survival 𝑉̃︀
𝒫(𝑡) from theorem 101
together with its current half-life estimate or bound. The single most important diagnostic is whether the
apparent portfolio resilience comes from genuine diversification or from averaging over benchmarks that
all share the same dominant decay channel. Theorem theorem 103 implies that common-mode leakage,
shared judge families, or shared generation pipelines can cap portfolio lifetime even when individual
benchmarks look superficially diverse.
The design question should also be reported, not merely the monitoring statistic. Theorem 104 shows
that with a single known deterministic hazard profile, horizon-survival optimization collapses to the single
best benchmark. Diversification becomes rational only once uncertainty sets, common-mode exposure
caps, or coverage requirements are declared. Theorems 107, 110, 111 and 118 to 120 then supply tractable
design problems together with explicit survival–coverage frontiers and robust coverage audits that should
be monitored rather than hidden inside ad hoc weights.
Operationally, this means benchmark families should be designed to reduce common-mode exposure as
well as to increase item count. Different provenance chains, different judge families, different generation
pipelines, and different task manifolds matter because they weaken common hazards, not because diversity
is aesthetically pleasing. It also means the report should include threshold invalidity times and, when a
scenario law is declared, safe quantiles or shortfall-tail summaries from theorems 137 to 139 rather than
a lone half-life. If monitoring is ongoing rather than one-shot, those summaries should be paired with
anytime-valid envelopes and stopping logic as in theorems 141, 143 and 144.
9.15 Coverage uncertainty and invalidity-risk reporting
Coverage should be audited as an uncertain matrix, not as a decorative table of point claims. In the
simplest case, one can elicit lower and upper coverage coefficients and report the box-robust design from
theorem 118. When coverage auditing is noisier or partly adversarial, a budgeted uncertainty model of
the form in theorem 119 is often more faithful: only some cells or benchmark–cell entries are allowed to
be maximally wrong at once, and the uncertainty budget Γ𝑔 is itself a governance parameter that should
be disclosed.
Reporting should also move beyond a single half-life scalar whenever the benchmark family supports
more than one operational threshold. For each declared threshold 𝛼, one should report the current lower
or interval estimate of 𝑇𝛼, together with at least one scenario-based safe quantile or shortfall-tail summary
from Definition 137. The point is not ornamentation. It is that a benchmark may look acceptable at
𝛼 = 1/2 while already being operationally unsafe at a stricter threshold or under the lower tail of the
scenario law.
88

=== PDF PAGE 89 ===
9.16 Dependence-aware learned coverage sets
When coverage audits are clustered by time, evaluator, benchmark family, or provenance chain, the learned
uncertainty set should itself be calibrated under that dependence structure. In practice this means first
declaring which regime from theorem 129 is supposed to justify the sequential set construction: a blockwise
or geometrically mixing regime, or a bounded-increment martingale regime. The resulting coordinatewise
confidence sequences should then be combined into the product set from theorem 130 rather than treating
the dependence correction as an afterthought. Once that is done, theorems 126 and 132 allow adaptive
monitoring or stopping-time portfolio updates without losing the coverage guarantee.
9.17 Nonstationary and regime-switching audit calibration
If audits are not merely dependent but also nonstationary, the protocol should stop pretending that one
immutable coverage vector governs all times. Instead it should declare either observable audit regimes or a
posterior-weighted regime filter and then maintain regime-specific confidence sequences as in theorems 133
and 134. Operationally, that means the audit log should record the regime label or filtering model,
the regime-specific sample allocation, and the rule used to map those regime-level intervals into the
current uncertainty set. Portfolio updates performed at stopping times should then be checked against the
appropriate observed-regime or posterior-mixture set, not against a stationary box lifted from a different
epoch.
9.18 Sequential monitoring and stopping
A live benchmark program should maintain an online upper and lower envelope for the validity quantity
that matters operationally. The exact construction may come from confidence sequences for pairwise
separation rates, anchor-agreement proportions, or portfolio survival summaries; the theory here only
requires the result to be anytime valid. Once such an envelope is in place, retirement can be triggered
by the upper-envelope crossing rule in theorem 143, continuation can be justified only while the lower
envelope remains above the governance threshold as in theorem 144, and the monitoring dashboard can
expose the life-cycle labels from theorem 145 rather than a raw score stream. This avoids the usual
p-hacking swamp where a benchmark is repeatedly audited until someone decides the answer looked
convenient enough to act on.
9.19 Dynamic retirement and regeneration planning
Monitoring alone does not choose actions. After constructing an anytime-valid envelope, the program
should declare the reward model, feasible regeneration actions, and safe action set used in the Bellman
recursion of theorem 150. In practical terms, this means recording the immediate operational benefit
of continued use, the direct cost of regeneration, and the expected state shift induced by candidate
regeneration policies. If the transition model is learned rather than known, the program should also
publish the ambiguity set used by the robust recursion in theorems 152 and 160; otherwise the planner is
merely a plug-in controller in the sense of theorem 153. Dominated regeneration actions should be pruned
before optimization using order arguments such as theorem 155. If exact safe dynamic programming
is computationally expensive, the program should report either direct value-approximation errors or
Bellman-residual certificates so that theorems 172, 173 and 175 can be invoked to translate solver error
into an explicit regret budget.
89

=== PDF PAGE 90 ===
9.20 Transition ambiguity sets for safe control
When the transition kernel in the safe-control layer is estimated from finite audit or intervention data,
the protocol should output an ambiguity set P𝑛(𝐼, 𝑎) or, under partial observability, a joint transition–
observation ambiguity set Q𝑛(𝑏, 𝑎) rather than a single plug-in kernel. In finite abstractions this can
be built directly from empirical confidence sets as in theorem 154; in richer spaces it can be built from
confidence rectangles, posterior credible regions with validated coverage, regime-specific uncertainty sets,
or declared distributional ambiguity families. What matters is that the construction be declared and that
the resulting family be rectangular enough for theorems 152 and 160 to apply. If only a nonrectangular
family is defensible, the protocol should publish a rectangular outer hull together with a pessimism-gap
budget in the sense of theorems 164 and 166. Reporting only the nominal planner hides model uncertainty
exactly where the action logic is most consequential. If only a nominal kernel is reported, it should be
labeled explicitly as a plug-in planner and compared against the robust lower value from theorem 153.
9.21 Belief-state control under partial observability
In many benchmark programs the true ecology state is not directly observed. The control object is then a
belief state built from audits, anchors, contamination signals, and regeneration logs. The protocol should
therefore declare the filtering model that maps histories into belief states and the lower-validity functional
that turns those beliefs into a safe action set, as formalized in theorems 156 to 158. If the dynamic model
is ambiguous, the report should also state whether the ambiguity is genuinely stagewise rectangular or
only available as a nonrectangular family. The former supports the exact robust Bellman recursion of
theorem 160; the latter should be accompanied by a reported rectangular outer hull and treated through
the conservative lower-value reduction of theorem 164, not smuggled into an “exact” Bellman story.
Without that declaration, talk of “state-aware” benchmark control is usually just a deterministic fairy
tale pasted over partial observability.
If the control action includes portfolio redesign, the estimation layer should additionally report
either certified lower coverage/survival summaries for each candidate action or a certified lower coverage
frontier 𝑣(𝜌; 𝑇). This lets the controller prune frontier-dominated actions using theorem 115 before
solving the dynamic program and prevents high-dimensional portfolio weights from leaking silently into a
low-transparency control layer.
9.22 Estimation-to-control handoff
The final output of the estimation layer should not be a pile of unrelated intervals. It should be a
control-ready certificate in the sense of theorem 177: an anytime-valid validity envelope, a declared
ambiguity set for the relevant transition law, and a solver certificate for the chosen planner. This is the
minimal interface needed for theorems 178 and 179 to convert statistical uncertainty into an operational
safety-and-value guarantee. A point estimate for validity plus a point estimate for transitions is not an
interface; it is wishful compression.
9.23 Exploration budgets for regeneration learning
When regeneration actions are learned online rather than fixed in advance, the protocol should report the
confidence system and optimism rule that govern exploration inside the safe action set. In practice this
90

=== PDF PAGE 91 ===
means specifying the belief-action abstraction, the confidence bonuses, and the resulting regret certificate
from theorems 167 to 169. Exploration that is not tied to an explicit width budget is not a learning
strategy; it is unpriced risk.
9.24 Retirement, regeneration, and scope restriction
The theory suggests a fail-closed rule:
Retire, regenerate, or scope-restrict a benchmark when the optimistic upper bound on current
validity falls below the governance threshold for the intended operational model class.
Operationally this is best exposed as the status map from theorem 145: active, watchlist, regenerate￾or-replace, or retire-or-scope-restrict. That labeling is not cosmetic. It forces the report to distinguish
epistemic ambiguity from demonstrated invalidity and to state whether a declared repair action actually
exists. Fix a threshold 𝜃 ∈ (0, 1). If the upper envelope in (72) satisfies
𝒱𝐵(0) 𝐽𝐵(𝑡) exp(︁
−
∫︁ 𝑡
0
𝑞𝐵
(𝑢) d𝑢
)︁
≤ 𝜃𝒱𝐵(0), (314)
then even the optimistic estimate is below threshold, and continued use for that operational class is no
longer justified. If the failure is frontier-specific rather than universal, scope restriction is preferable to
binary retirement.
10 Toy Regimes and Phase Structure
A full empirical calibration is outside the scope of the present paper, but the state-variable theory already
implies a useful phase portrait. Consider the passive linear proxy
𝑣˙𝑡 = −
[︁
𝜆𝑠𝑡 + 𝜌𝑗𝑡 + 𝛾𝑘𝑡 + 𝜑(1 − 𝑓𝑡) + 𝜓(1 − 𝑢𝑡) − 𝜂𝑛𝑡
]︁
+
𝑣𝑡
, 𝑣𝑡
:= 𝒱𝐵(𝑡), (315)
with state variables evolving more slowly than 𝑣𝑡 between declared regime times. Upward validity
movements are represented by declared regeneration jumps, not by negative passive hazard.
Regime I: Static-decay regime. If 𝑛𝑡
is near zero while 𝑠𝑡
, 𝑗𝑡
, 𝑘𝑡
, or support erosion remain positive,
the net decay rate stays positive and the benchmark has a short finite half-life. This approximates
long-lived public benchmarks with limited refresh and increasing recursive exposure.
Regime II: Regenerated-stable regime. If verified novelty inflow is sufficiently strong and incoming
items remain genuinely distinct and verifiable, the passive hazard can be driven close to zero and scheduled
regeneration jumps can restore validity after auditing. This is the design target of dynamic benchmarks.
Regime III: False-stability regime. The benchmark appears alive because scores still move, but the
movement is driven by evaluator coupling or benchmark-specific exploitability rather than the intended
capability. Here discriminative power may remain nontrivial while construct validity deteriorates. Raw
leaderboard motion becomes epistemically misleading.
91

=== PDF PAGE 92 ===
Regime IV: Class-relative saturation regime. A benchmark can lose frontier discriminative power
while retaining narrower diagnostic or pedagogical value for weaker systems. In this case the benchmark
is not universally dead; it has a shorter half-life for one operational class than for another, as formalized
in theorem 37. This is why Π𝑡 matters.
These regimes suggest a phase diagram with horizontal axis 𝑁𝑡 and vertical axis effective decay pressure
𝑆𝑡 + 𝜆𝐽𝑡 + 𝜇𝐾𝑡 + 𝜈(1 − 𝐹𝑡) + 𝜉(1 − 𝑈𝑡). Regeneration moves left-to-right; uncontrolled recursive exposure
moves bottom-to-top.
Regime V: Portfolio-fragile regime. A benchmark family appears diversified at the item level, but
most of its components share the same judge family, exposure channels, or construction pipeline. Then
the portfolio behaves as though it were a single instrument with a strong common-mode hazard, and
the apparent breadth does little to extend the family half-life. This is the portfolio analogue of hidden
evaluator coupling.
Known-best versus robustly-diversified portfolios. If one benchmark is known to dominate all
others at the target horizon, then deterministic optimization puts all weight on that benchmark. A
diversified portfolio becomes rational only when the hazard profile is uncertain, when common-mode
exposure must be capped, or when governance requires breadth across task manifolds. This sounds obvious
once stated, but scoreboards often behave as though diversification were self-justifying. It is not.
Analytic Example 1: Constant hazard with a certified jump envelope. Suppose 𝑞
lin
𝐵 (𝑡) ≡ 𝑞 > 0
on [0, 𝑇] and the declared cumulative jump lower envelope is 𝐽
safe
𝐵 (𝑡) ≡ 𝑒
−Λ for all 𝑡 ≤ 𝑇. Then theorem 34
yields
𝒱𝐵(𝑡) ≥ 𝒱𝐵(0)𝑒
−(𝑞𝑡+Λ)

Hence 𝜏1/2

𝑇𝛼(𝐵) > 𝑇 whenever 𝑞𝑇 + Λ < log(1/𝛼). This is the simplest closed-form illustration of how passive
decay and jump risk add on the log scale.

diversification appears only when scenario uncertainty makes equalization valuable.

11 Implications for Benchmark Governance
11.1 From scoreboards to validity budgets
If benchmark validity is time-varying and hazard-driven, then a leaderboard score without a validity
budget is incomplete. A benchmark report should ideally include estimated leakage, regeneration rate,
verification fidelity, evaluator coupling, anchor-based construct-validity intervals, a current validity interval,
and, where portfolio redesign is live, a certified coverage frontier or certified lower coverage/survival
summaries for the action class. This is closer to instrument reporting in mature empirical sciences than to
static scoreboards.
11.2 Portfolio governance and common-mode audits
Benchmark families should be governed as portfolios, not as piles of unrelated scoreboards. Reporting
only per-benchmark validity can hide the more important question: whether the family is exposed to a
dominant common-mode decay channel. A useful portfolio report therefore includes the weighted survival
curve, the portfolio half-life, and a qualitative or quantitative audit of common hazards such as shared
judges, shared construction pipelines, shared retrieval corpora, or shared benchmark-aware communities.
11.3 Portfolio design should state its scenario set
A benchmark portfolio should disclose why its weights are what they are. If the weights follow a single
deterministic survival estimate, then the implied design should usually be sparse or even concentrated
by theorem 104. If the design is diversified, the report should specify the scenario set, uncertainty
model, exposure-cap logic, and coverage targets that justify diversification, ideally in the explicit forms
of theorems 106 and 109. Otherwise portfolio weighting becomes decorative rather than epistemically
meaningful.
11.4 Coverage claims should carry uncertainty budgets
Coverage matrices are often treated as if they were ground truth. Usually they are not. They are outputs
of taxonomies, audits, or expert judgments and can be wrong in structured ways. If a portfolio’s breadth
claim depends on those matrices, the report should state whether coverage is nominal, fixed-robust, or
learned-robust in the senses of theorems 117 to 119, 121 and 122. Otherwise “coverage” becomes another
scoreboard number with hidden fragility.
93

=== PDF PAGE 94 ===
11.5 Dependence-aware audit sets should match the sampling reality
If coverage matrices are learned from clustered or time-dependent audits, then the uncertainty set should
be valid under that dependence pattern rather than under a fictional i.i.d. story. A learned set with
poor dependence handling is just a brittle confidence costume. The dependence-aware transfer theorem
theorem 126 makes the governance requirement explicit: adaptive monitoring is fine, but only after the
uncertainty set itself has a uniform validity guarantee under the actual audit process.
11.6 Half-life is not the only lifetime scalar that matters
A half-life is one threshold at one loss level. Operational governance often needs a stricter question: by
when does the benchmark become invalid under the declared threshold and uncertainty law? Reporting
threshold invalidity times and lower safe quantiles makes the failure mode legible in ways a single 𝜏1/2
cannot. When tail failures matter, shortfall-tail summaries are often the honest object.
11.7 Sequential retirement should be anytime-valid
Benchmark programs are usually monitored repeatedly, not inspected once and sealed in amber. A
retirement or continuation decision therefore should be tied to an anytime-valid envelope, not to a one-shot
interval that quietly assumes nobody peeked early. The safe default is simple: retire only when the upper
envelope crosses the governance threshold, continue only while the lower envelope remains above it, and
state the error budget attached to that online rule.
11.8 Retirement and regeneration should be optimized inside a safe action set
A benchmark program should not treat retirement and regeneration as ad hoc committee rituals. Once an
anytime-valid envelope exists, the admissible action set should be declared up front and the retirement–
regeneration trade-off should be optimized inside that safe region as in theorems 148 and 150. Otherwise
the program is doing dynamic control while pretending it is merely “monitoring.”
11.9 Public versus private is not enough
Hiding test sets can reduce some exposure channels, but saturation evidence suggests that privacy alone
does not robustly prevent lifetime erosion [20]. In the present framework, privacy affects leakage only
indirectly; if semantic derivatives, benchmark-aware communities, or evaluator coupling remain high,
durability still degrades.
11.10 Dynamic evaluation must be metrologically constrained
A badly designed dynamic benchmark can create novelty without validity. Regeneration that lacks prove￾nance, verification, or construct alignment simply replaces one failure mode with another. Regeneration is
therefore not a synonym for quality; it is a control input whose value depends on 𝐹𝑡
, 𝐿𝑡
, and 𝐾𝑡 as well as
on 𝑁𝑡
.
94

=== PDF PAGE 95 ===
11.11 Progress claims should be temporally indexed
A benchmark score is a claim about a model relative to a benchmark at a particular validity state. In
recursive corpora, scores are not timeless properties. They are conditional on benchmark age, exposure
history, maintenance quality, and the operational model class under consideration.
12 Limitations
This is a theory paper and therefore has the usual vices if left unbounded by discipline. First, the state
variables are abstractions, and real estimators for them can be noisy, model-dependent, or incomplete. In
particular, a small construct-validity component can come from a small resolved-pair mass, poor order
fidelity on resolved pairs, or both. Those cases should not be collapsed into one vague scalar story;
the resolved-mass estimate and resolution-status label must be reported alongside the scalar. Second,
the target-claim functional 𝐺𝑡
is partly conceptual and partly domain-specific; construct validity is
therefore only as good as the declared claim and the external anchors used to audit it. Third, the shared
state-reduced exact law is a modeling assumption rather than a theorem of nature; only the envelope
results avoid that stronger commitment, and the interval-propagation theorem is conditional on a justified
hazard map. Fourth, the factor-specific exact law is stronger still, and factor-specific hazard identification
requires separate access to 𝒟𝐵 and 𝒞𝐵 together with calibrated ecological-state recovery and sufficiently
rich support; aggregate validity alone does not suffice. Fifth, the linear proxy is a governance convenience,
not a universal law. Sixth, although the theory deliberately avoids treating origin labels as primitives,
provenance metadata can still be useful auxiliary evidence in broader estimators. The claim here is only
that origin labels are not sufficient primitives for benchmark governance. Seventh, the interval results
inherit whatever errors or misspecification exist in the calibration correspondence or state-bracketing
procedure. Eighth, even in the strengthened empirical protocol, many settings will remain set identified
rather than point identified, and matched interventions generally recover effect signs more readily than
effect magnitudes. Ninth, hazard-map identification is inherently local to calibrated and sufficiently
excited portions of state space; off-support extrapolations remain structural assumptions even when they
look visually smooth. The new extrapolation results do not repeal that fact: they require declared shape
restrictions such as a justified Lipschitz modulus or monotone order, and those restrictions may themselves
be wrong. Tenth, anchor-panel aggregation can be much less informative than its panel size suggests
when common blind spots or positive dependence are strong, and latent-factor bounds can still be loose
when the factor itself is badly measured. Eleventh, factor-specific intervention design helps most under
genuinely justified exclusive regimes; shared-coordinate interventions still need separate factor traces,
credible exclusion restrictions, or a full-rank local design matrix. Under imperfect exclusion or invalid-IV
style direct effects, the theory honestly widens the answer into a sensitivity region, but that region is only
as meaningful as the declared violation budgets. Twelfth, coverage matrices are often uncertain objects
in their own right, and robust coverage frontiers are only as meaningful as the auditing process that
produced the uncertainty set. Learned uncertainty sets improve honesty but do not abolish sampling bias,
taxonomy drift, or audit misspecification; their guarantees are only as good as the finite-sample coverage
statement they actually satisfy. Thirteenth, time-to-invalidity risk summaries inherit whatever scenario
law, threshold choice, and tail-risk functional one declares; a fancy CVaR number can still be nonsense if
the scenario set is decorative. Fourteenth, sequential monitoring gains validity only if the online envelope
is genuinely anytime valid for the estimator and dependence structure in use; otherwise continuous peeking
95

=== PDF PAGE 96 ===
turns the stopping rule back into theater. Fifteenth, the new sharp imperfect-exclusion results are only as
informative as the declared violation class and its attainability logic; a narrow identified set based on
fantasy exclusions is still fantasy. Sixteenth, dependence-aware learned uncertainty sets still take a valid
uniform set-coverage guarantee as an input; they do not conjure that guarantee from arbitrary dependence
without additional empirical work. Seventeenth, the dynamic retirement–regeneration control layer is
only as good as the declared transition kernel and reward model. Bellman equations do not rescue bad
operational assumptions. Eighteenth, the new structural-sharpness results for imperfect exclusion contract
identified sets only if the structural violation class is actually credible; sign, monotonicity, sparsity, or
linear-coupling restrictions that are merely wished into existence can make the theory look sharper than
the evidence warrants. Nineteenth, the mixing and martingale audit regimes have now been extended to
observed-regime and posterior-mixture nonstationary audits, but long-memory or adversarially drifting
audit processes can still defeat the declared confidence construction. Twentieth, safe POMDP control is
only as good as the declared filtering model and observation kernel, while robust POMDP value guarantees
additionally depend on the truth lying inside the reported ambiguity set; a polished belief state built from
a wrong hidden-state model is still a wrong state. Twenty-first, nondifferential directional anchor error is
a real sharpening but not a free lunch: if anchor mistakes depend directly on the benchmark comparator
even after conditioning on the latent target order, the affine correction in theorem 85 can be wrong and
the honest fallback is the looser partial-identification logic from theorems 83 and 93. Twenty-second,
rectangular-hull planning under nonrectangular ambiguity is conservative by design; its value lower bound
can be materially looser than the true nonrectangular robust value when impossible kernel combinations
make the hull much larger than the actually admissible family. Twenty-third, optimistic regeneration
learning controls regret only on the confidence event and only on the chosen safe belief-action abstraction;
hidden confounding, unpriced drift, or undercoverage in the bonuses can make the reported exploration
budget fiction. Twenty-fourth, approximate safe control bounds certify solver error only relative to the
declared model class and reachable information states; hidden model misspecification can dominate the
reported regret bound. Twenty-fifth, the grid-based approximation theorem assumes Lipschitz regularity of
rewards and transitions in a chosen metric, and those assumptions can fail badly when benchmark ecology
changes discontinuously. Twenty-sixth, portfolio-level lifetime gains vanish under large common-mode
hazards, and portfolio diversification is only as meaningful as the declared uncertainty, exposure, and
coverage model. The framework is therefore fail-closed only to the extent that its uncertainty envelopes,
dependence budgets, shape restrictions, exclusion logic, learned uncertainty sets, scenario declarations,
and control models are themselves honestly constructed.
13 Conclusion
Benchmarks now live inside recursive corpora. In that environment they are not static mirrors of capability
but decaying instruments whose usefulness depends on semantic leakage, evaluator coupling, construct drift,
support erosion, verification fidelity, and regeneration. The right primitive is therefore not nominal data
origin but benchmark validity, defined here as the product of discriminative power and a construct-validity
component that incorporates both resolved-pair mass and thresholded order fidelity, and governed by a
state-dependent hazard. That hazard induces a benchmark half-life.
The resulting picture is neither fatalistic nor naive. Benchmarks can and often do decay under persistent
exposure pressure. But regeneration, verifiability, provenance-aware auditing, and deliberate metrological
96

=== PDF PAGE 97 ===
design can extend or stabilize their useful life. The scientific implication is direct: benchmark results
should be interpreted together with a life-cycle state, not as timeless capability facts. The identification
implication is equally direct: aggregate validity, factor-specific hazards, anchor-based order-fidelity and
construct-validity intervals, and portfolio survival do not become visible under the same information regime,
so reports should state which regime they inhabit. The engineering implication is equally direct: benchmark
governance must move from static scoreboards to dynamic validity management, with identification status,
dependence budgets, exclusion logic, coverage uncertainty budgets, estimation-to-control certificates, and
scenario declarations reported explicitly rather than smuggled in as false precision. Half-life remains useful,
but it should sit inside a wider family of threshold invalidity times and tail-risk summaries whenever
operational failure thresholds differ across use cases. Once monitoring becomes live, the governance
problem is no longer only inferential; it is also sequential and control-theoretic. Nonstationary audits may
require regime-indexed or posterior-mixture uncertainty sets rather than one frozen coverage matrix, and
partially observed benchmark ecologies require belief-state rather than fairy-tale fully observed control.
Safe benchmark programs therefore need three things at once: honest uncertainty sets, honest action sets,
and honest learning budgets for regeneration. When those action rules are implemented approximately or
learned online, the approximation and exploration error should itself be metrologized through Bellman
residuals, reachable-state error bounds, confidence widths, or explicit regret certificates rather than hidden
behind solver mystique.
14 Suggested Empirical Program
A concrete empirical follow-up could proceed in eleven stages.
1. Select benchmark families with long public exposure histories and enough leaderboard data to estimate
changing discriminative power.
2. Reconstruct approximate recursive corpora from web snapshots, tutorials, solution repositories, public
evaluation harnesses, model-generated discussions, and benchmark-aware prompting traces.
3. Estimate exact, lexical, semantic, and variant leakage side by side to quantify the gap between
exact-match and semantic hazard proxies.
4. When feasible, separately track discriminative-power and construct-validity traces so that factor-specific
hazards can be identified or bounded rather than conflated into a single composite hazard.
5. Build time-indexed validity envelopes by combining score-separation statistics, construct-audit scores,
judge-coupling proxies, and provenance observability indicators.
6. Maintain at least one dependence-robust anchor panel for each major benchmark family and document
whether the dependence budget is framed by conditional independence, shared blind spots, covariance
bounds, or a latent-factor model.
7. For shared-coordinate interventions, elicit asymmetric or sign-restricted spillover classes and report
sharp identified sets rather than only symmetric sensitivity intervals.
8. Learn coverage uncertainty sets under the actual audit dependence structure and verify stopping-time
validity before using those sets for adaptive portfolio updates.
97

=== PDF PAGE 98 ===
9. For benchmark families, publish the scenario set and exposure caps that justify portfolio weights, rather
than presenting diversification as self-justifying.
10. Estimate or otherwise conservatively bracket regeneration transition kernels and solve the safe Bellman
control problem for retirement versus regeneration under declared utility and cost models.
11. Compare static and dynamic benchmarks by estimated half-life, threshold invalidity times, and life-cycle
state rather than by instantaneous score spread alone.
This program would not merely test the theory. It would produce the first benchmark-life-cycle dashboards
suitable for real governance and safe action planning.
